F1 in Singapore: There's a new fastest car now
2026-10-12

F1 in Singapore: There's a new fastest car now

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-12

Visit Original Website
Formula 1’s lengthy 2026 season is entering its final stretch. The sport took to the floodlit streets of Singapore on Sunday night, but Saturday saw action, too; this Grand Prix weekend was also the final of six sprints this year. The night race might be one of modern F1’s greatest spectacles. From overhead shots, the track becomes a bright, illuminated ribbon among the looming darkness of the city-state’s overpasses and skyscrapers. At ground level, the cars shower glittering sparks from their titanium skid blocks as the drivers flirt with how close they can get to the walls. As tracks go, the 90-degree turns and concrete barrier-lined streets were a world away from the abrasive tarmac and ample runoff of Malaysia last time out. But the tropical heat and humidity were a constant given both locations’ proximity to the equator. In Malaysia (which was still technically the Bahrain GP), a pre-race downpour disrupted things mightily. On Saturday morning in Singapore, shortly before the start of the sprint, it seemed history was about to rhyme when the skies opened. With standing water in places on the track, the sprint was delayed by 30 minutes. The sprint format, where a short extra race replaces a practice session a day before the main event, is unloved by the drivers and teams. For one thing there’s little prestige in winning Saturday’s sprint. And with qualifying just a few hours later, risk-taking is discouraged lest one wreck the car and ruin Sunday’s race a day early. And in Singapore, like most street circuits, the proximity of the unyielding barriers that line the track mean there are real, punishing consequences for getting it even a little wrong. Which is what happened to George Russell. The Mercedes driver went into this season as the title favorite, only to see those hopes evaporated by a combination of car unreliability and a young teammate, Kimi Antonelli, who really does appear to be a generational talent. A win in Azerbaijan in September was a chance for Russell to get back on terms with Antonelli, but he failed to finish in Malaysia, and his sprint didn’t last much longer. It started well. The track was still far too wet for slick tires, so the entire field lined up on intermediate treaded tires. Russell’s Mercedes made a much better start from second on the grid than polesitter Max Verstappen’s Red Bull, and he stole the lead. A lap later, Russell’s sprint was over, and his car bent and broken after he misjudged the final corner. On a wet race track, any painted sections become very slippery, something that goes double for street circuits. “I went a little bit wide, and then I touched the white line. The No. 1 rule in the rain is don’t touch the white line. It’s a very silly mistake–overly confident. I think it’s my first crash of the year. It’s annoying to happen from that position,” Russell said after the fact. Verstappen benefited from Russell’s mistake, inheriting the lead. For a while, Lewis Hamilton’s Ferrari appeared to be a threat. But intermediate tires will begin to overheat as the track dries, something that affected Hamilton and the other Ferrari of Charles Leclerc before long. At the end of 20 laps, he crossed the finish line first. Surprisingly, this was Verstappen’s first victory in Singapore. Although, as mentioned, he didn’t exactly see it as such. “I want to win tomorrow,” Verstappen said after the race. “I don’t see this as a win in terms of actually winning a Grand Prix, you know. So we need to have a good qualifying. It will be very close with a lot of cars, I think, and we just need to try and make sure, of course, we are the best one out of them. But it will be, yeah, hopefully a good battle.” His Red Bull was recently updated, and in combination with a power unit that has the strongest V6 in the field (if perhaps not the best hybrid system) it may well be the fastest car on the grid, at least in Verstappen’s capable hands. A few hours later, he secured pole position in qualifying for Sunday’s race. Like the sprint finishing order, he would have the two Ferraris behind him, albeit with Leclerc ahead of Hamilton. It was a lights-to-flag victory for Verstappen, even with the added complication of radio troubles—he could hear his engineer, but his replies were unintelligible. Such was his pace and tire management that he was able to stop for tires and leave the pits without giving up the lead. “The car was really good on the tires. I could really manage them well. But to be honest, I think it was one of the first times that I’ve driven in clean air here in Singapore in the race, so I guess that’s also nice. And yeah, it’s been just a fantastic weekend for us,” Verstappen said. The last three races have seen two wins and a second for Verstappen. “I really think, of course, that our car has taken a really nice step forward. But at the end of the day, yeah, it’s just been more driveable and more predictable. That’s what I wanted and needed from the car to find that last step to fight up front,” he said. A newly resurgent Verstappen, currently in fourth place, could be a threat for second place in the standings by season’s end. Not that he really cares. “I hope that we can carry that momentum forward. We don’t really have anything to win in the championship, right? I think Kimi is miles ahead. For me personally, I don’t care if I’m second, third, or fourth. Like, yeah, it doesn’t do anything. But I just want to have fun till the end, you know, try to go for a day’s success,” he said. With both sprint and race poles, and sprint and race victories, it was almost a perfect weekend for Verstappen, who only missed out on fastest lap to Leclerc, who came home second. It should have been a double podium for Ferrari. Hamilton did not have as much speed as Leclerc and started the race on an alternate strategy, using the medium tires instead of the softs. These didn’t last long though, and Hamilton stopped on lap 17, switching to the softs. With most of the 62-lap race left to run, that set up the potential for a very long afternoon. And when Lance Stroll’s Aston Martin broke down as usual and caused a virtual safety car period, inexplicably Hamilton overrode the team’s instructions to follow Leclerc into the pits to solve the problem by making a cheap stop. Staying out on increasingly ruined tires left the seven-time champion a sitting duck to cars behind, and he turned third into sixth, apologizing to his team on the radio afterward. As we have discussed more than once this season, perhaps the cardinal rule as an F1 driver is “don’t hit your teammate.” That we’ve seen teammates battling a lot isn’t entirely unsurprising—if the cars are stratified by pace, and a team has two drivers of about the same caliber, then if they both perform well, they should qualify in similar positions and therefore find themselves racing together. Haas’ Oliver Bearman showed the right way to do that, going side by side with his teammate Esteban Ocon for several corners on lap 3, a battle that was sadly relegated to a small Picture in Picture window on the broadcast. (A couple of laps later, Bearman followed it with a late move on Carlos Sainz’ Williams, too.) Sometimes, we see situations like Ferrari has faced in recent races, including this one, splitting strategies between two cars but with the car on newer, faster tires stuck behind the car on older, slower rubber. Sometimes the driver in front does what’s best for the team and lets them by without a fight—Hamilton did just this late in the race when a much faster Leclerc on much newer tires needed to get by. What no fan—or team boss—wants to see are both their cars trying to take the same piece of tarmac. Which is exactly what happened with McLaren’s pair during Saturday’s sprint. The orange cars were in fourth (Piastri) and fifth (Norris) on the last lap of the race when Norris decided to try to invert that order. The pair made contact, Piastri’s car broke, and Norris finished eighth. Nine points for the team turned into one point, and Norris had given the team extra work to do to repair the other car in time for qualifying. McLaren’s team principal, Andrea Stella, called the incident “unacceptable” and presumably read the riot act to his driver. Norris was suitably chastened the following day, both offering to help Piastri in the race if he could, and telling journalists after the race that “I can’t attempt what I did. I should never have attempted it in the first place, even if the move was on. I’ve simply got to think more of the team that I represent rather than myself. It was a mistake on my part, and I let down the team,” Norris said. With this Asian leg complete, the F1 circus now relocates to the Americas, starting with the Circuit of the Americas in Austin, Texas. There’s a chance Antonelli could clinch the title if he leaves Texas 125 points ahead of his closest rival. Currently, that’s George Russell, who trails by 102 points, with Hamilton in third at 111 behind, and Verstappen 119 points behind Antonelli in fourth. A win is worth 25 points, so it’s possible, but it would require yet more bad luck from Russell; by my admittedly terrible arithmetic, if Russell finishes ninth and only scores two points to an Antonelli win, his title hopes are over. But if he finishes eighth and scores four points, or does better, his hopes stay alive at least until the following round in Mexico. Similar elimination calculations exist to rule out Hamilton and Verstappen, with higher finishing positions required to stay in it due to their greater points deficits to the leader. Given Verstappen’s form recently, and Mercedes’ inability to make its own upgrade work quite so well, there’s every prospect of a third consecutive Red Bull win in a couple of weeks. And Lewis Hamilton has finished on the podium at COTA nine times now, so he knows how to find a result there, even if a Ferrari victory seems unlikely. Ars will be on the ground in Austin, where I’m hoping to learn more about how the teams program these fiendishly complicated power units and see who’s prepared to talk about those carbon-neutral synthetic fuels the sport was so keen to tell me about just a few short years ago.

Google announces the Fitbit Edge, a middle ground between Pixel Watch 5 and Fitbit Air
2026-10-12

Google announces the Fitbit Edge, a middle ground between Pixel Watch 5 and Fitbit Air

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-12

Visit Original Website
Google is releasing its second Fitbit tracker of 2026, offering fans of the aging Charge 6 a more refined option. The new Fitbit Edge slots right in between the Pixel Watch 5 and the Fitbit Air, featuring a compact screen and more health sensors than the Air. You won’t get all the apps and features of the Pixel Watch 5, but the Edge is only about half as expensive. The Fitbit Edge is essentially a new Charge 6, but it has more of a Pixel Watch aesthetic. Squish Google’s smartwatch from the sides, and you’ve got the Edge. It has a pill-shaped 1.34-inch OLED touch display inside a slender aluminum frame that is rated IP68 (5 ATM) for water and dust protection. Google says the screen has 46 percent more viewable area compared to the Charge 6. It’s very close in overall size to the Fitbit Air and uses the same magnetic charging puck. The Edge works with Android or iPhones via the Google Health app, and it can run for about seven days on a charge. If the tracker is away from your phone, it can still follow your activity all week with support for seven days of full health data storage (or 30 days of basic data). However, the generous week-long battery life does not include always-on display mode. If you choose to turn that on, the battery life drops to about two days, barely longer than the Pixel Watch 5. The Fitbit Edge plays it right down the middle in Google’s wearable lineup, and not just when it comes to price and size—button count also splits the difference. The Pixel Watch has two buttons, the Air has none, and the Edge sports a single tactile side button. You can use that to wake the device or start/pause workouts. Most interactions will be handled with the touchscreen, though. The Edge will ship with a collection of fitness-focused experiences and watch faces, and you won’t be able to download more. While this looks a lot like the Pixel Watch’s software, it’s not a full version of WearOS. It can mirror notifications from your phone, but there’s no microphone or speaker. So responses are limited to pre-defined quick replies. Likewise, there’s no support for Gemini interactions. The Edge also lacks NFC, so no Google Wallet tap-to-pay. One notable upgrade over the Fitbit Air is on-device GPS. This allows you to track an outdoor activity without bringing your phone along for the ride. Although, Google says it’s not as accurate as the dual-band GPS radio in the Pixel Watch. The Edge does retain the ECG and blood oxygen sensor from the Pixel Watch, which you don’t get on the Air. The Edge also has an altimeter, optical heart rate sensor, and skin temperature sensor. All this data is beamed over Bluetooth 5.4 to the Google Health app (previously Fitbit), feeding all your data into the Gemini-powered Health Coach chatbot. The device comes with three months of Google Health Premium, without which you won’t get the AI features. Of course, some would probably prefer to skip the AI. The Premium subscription is $10 per month. It’s included with Google AI Pro, but you can disable the Health Coach if you want. Like the Pixel Watch, the Edge will come with the basic silicone Active Band for $180. There is also a Stephen Curry Special Edition available for $220. The Edge has a custom band connector similar to the Pixel Watch, but the plug is a different size. While there will eventually be third-party options, Google plans to have a line of add-on bands at launch, including the Active Sport (silicone with holes) for $40, Performance Loop (polyester fabric) for $50, Magnet Band (leather-ish but still silicone) at $60, and a Metal Links Band for a whopping $150. The Fitbit Edge is available for preorder today and will ship on October 27. It comes in matte black, polished silver, satin pyrite (between silver and gold), and champagne gold. While this is a much more expensive tracker compared to the $100 Fitbit Air, it’s only a little more than the Charge 6’s 2023 launch price of $160. The Pixel Watch 5 starts at $350. Like the Air, this tracker is not an island within Google’s ecosystem. You can pair multiple Google wearables with the Health app and wear whichever one you want. There’s no need to re-pair or manually switch your health tracking over. The app will collect stats from the device you wear at any given time, changing as often as necessary. So you could sport the Pixel Watch 5 during the day and the Edge when you hit the gym, for example. Google also notes the Edge can stay with you for the long haul. This is the company’s first repairable fitness tracker, which has been designed with a replaceable battery. Note, that does not mean user-replaceable. There will be service options to swap the battery, but Google still doesn’t recommend you open up the tracker yourself.

Amazon has over 1 million robot workers. What’s next for human employees?
2026-10-12

Amazon has over 1 million robot workers. What’s next for human employees?

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-12

Visit Original Website
No organization in the world operates more robots than Amazon. The company already has more than 1 million robots working in warehouses around the clock to make tens of millions of same-day and one-day package deliveries happen on a daily basis—and Amazon is poised to significantly grow that robotic fleet in the coming years as it establishes new factories to churn out increasingly more capable robot workers. Despite deploying so many robots, Amazon is also the second-largest corporate employer in the world, with more than 1.5 million full-time and part-time human workers, according to its 2025 annual report. Amazon executives say that the growing robot fleet has eased the burden on its human workforce by taking on the toughest fulfillment center tasks that carry the greatest risks of physical fatigue and injury. But labor advocacy groups and independent researchers point to evidence suggesting that warehouse automation driven by AI is not all upside for the humans who work alongside robots. “For the last 100 years, most of the automation that we’ve seen usually happens away from workers—you’re taking that high-risk task away from the worker, and you’re doing so in a way that is not physically proximate,” Brian Greenwood, professor of business at George Mason University, told Ars. “The rise of collaborative robots really changes that dynamic for two reasons: one is that it is physically proximate, and two, the robot has the ability to dictate the pace of work.” In many ways, robotics and AI are well suited to the relentless pace of work that is already happening inside e-commerce fulfillment centers. Amazon’s promise of speedy deliveries for its 200 million Prime subscribers in particular requires hundreds of thousands of human employees to speedily assemble and ship out customer orders that often combine a dizzying array of products. Much of the work in fulfillment centers also frequently involves “dull, dirty and dangerous” tasks that people may prefer to hand over to robots. The injury and illness rate for workers in the US warehouse sector has typically been more than double the average across all industries, according to the US Occupational Safety and Health Administration. “You have almost 2 million people working in the warehousing sector in the United States, and it’s characterized by very high turnover and very high workplace injury rates,” said Greenwood. But robots are still far from capable of handling the many different tasks that human workers can perform. So Amazon still has a massive human workforce working in tandem with the company’s growing fleet of wheeled robots and robotic arms—and workplaces can get complicated when humans are expected to keep pace with robotic counterparts. Amazon has rapidly built its robotic fleet following the pivotal decision to acquire the robotics company Kiva Systems in 2012. Kiva was among the earliest companies to pioneer wheeled mobile robots that bring racks of items to warehouse workers, instead of requiring people to continually walk around grabbing items off of shelves inside fulfillment centers. Since the acquisition, Kiva was renamed Amazon Robotics and has focused on deploying an array of mobile robots and robotic arms that Amazon primarily manufactures in-house at facilities in Massachusetts. This year, the company also announced it would build additional production factories in Austin, Texas, and Greenwood, Indiana, to churn out even more robots in the future. “There’s a theme of how we look at the challenges that we can help our associates and our employees with, and how we can make our customers’ lives better through these technologies,” Scott Dresser, vice president of Amazon Robotics, told Ars. “Each step of the way, [we are] tackling a hard or deep set of technical challenges to achieve that goal.” Most of Amazon’s million-robot fleet still consists of those wheeled mobile robots. The company claims that its robotic Sequoia system can identify and store items up to 75 percent faster at fulfillment centers by coordinating swarms of such robots. Hercules and Titan robots, the size of a pool table or a baby grand piano, respectively, roam warehouse aisles to retrieve tall fabric shelving units called pods packed with plastic tote crates and bring them to human employees at various workstations. Such mobile robots typically follow grid lines of stickers laid out on the floor and stay within fenced-in areas away from human employees. But the newest mobile robot, called Proteus, has sensors that allow it to operate safely around humans and other objects in a more free-roaming role. Those swarms of mobile robots traveling through narrow lanes between rows of storage pods can also sometimes create robotic traffic jams. That is why Amazon began coordinating its robots using a suite of AI foundation models collectively named DeepFleet, which can help predict potential robot congestion ahead of time. More recently, Amazon has been deploying a growing number of robotic arms that can grab items stowed high up inside inventory pods, sort packages and place them in carts or on top of mobile robots, and handle the many different individual products that Amazon customers order when shopping online. The company expects to more than double its existing fleet of robotic arms this year, according to Amazon CFO Brian Olsavsky in a July earnings call. When Ars sought clarification, Dresser described the current number of deployed robotic arms as being in the single-digit thousands. Amazon first bought third-party robotic arms to help lift and stack heavy pallets and bins inside its fulfillment centers. But the first robotic arm developed in-house by Amazon Robotics to sort individual mailer envelopes and boxes from cluttered piles, called Robin, began testing in 2021 before debuting at a facility in Lakeland, Florida, in 2022. “With Robin, it was really that first attempt at taking a number of customer orders all in a jumble and building the advanced robotics to identify the items in that scene, to strategize on how to pick them up and build a motion plan that would be successful in placing it down on one of our mobile drive units,” Dresser explained. The robotic arm uses eight suction cups to pick up individual packages. But before it takes any action, the robot’s software analyzes a top-down view of the conveyor belt cluttered with packages and quickly ranks possible picking actions. In a 2023 research paper, the Amazon Robotics team described having developed several picking strategies for the robots to follow early on—but each had its own limitations. One strategy that prioritized picking larger packages encountered challenges when smaller packages lay on top of or overlapped with larger ones. Another strategy that favored picking up packages near the top of piles would fail in practice to retrieve “unreachable picks” and partially buried packages. Improvements came from replacing such hand-crafted algorithms with an AI approach known as machine learning. The machine learning model initially trained on more than 394,000 picking actions from deployed robotic arms to estimate pick probabilities of success, and then used an online pick ranker to prioritize optimal actions for the Robin robots. The best machine-learning method in that trial achieved a 93.73 percent success rate compared to 92.28 percent success for the best-performing traditional algorithm. That may seem trivial at first glance, but it meant the machine-learning method resulted in about 16,500 fewer failed picks than the traditional method during a work period involving more than 1 million total picks. That matters for an operation like Amazon’s that prioritizes speed and efficiency at scale. The Amazon Robotics team further showed how such a machine-learning method could reduce the failed pick rate by 20 percent during an experiment involving 2 million picks by robotic workstations set up to resemble the operations of Amazon’s Robin fleet. They presented this work at the International Symposium on Experimental Robotics in July 2025. Next up was Cardinal, a robotic arm that can place packages weighing up to 50 pounds into wheeled carts that are eventually brought to Amazon trucks waiting in loading docks. These robots first began operating at a facility in Nashville, Tennessee, in November 2022. “Think of it as a game of Tetris: You’re sorting packages, and you’re placing them into the cart, and you want to be able to pack that as densely as you possibly can,” Dresser told Ars. “Dense containers are great from a sustainability perspective and from a transportation cost perspective.” Cardinal built on lessons learned from Robin’s development in picking up and manipulating packages, while adding the capability to densely pack customer orders into the carts. Amazon has just started to deploy Cardinal in larger numbers and will continue to do so over the next several years, Dresser said. Robin and Cardinal can handle several dozen package variations. But the newer Sparrow robotic arm was developed to tackle an even more challenging task—handling the hundreds of millions of products of varying shapes and sizes that can go into customer orders. Specifically, Sparrow focuses on consolidating items from partially filled container-size totes to create densely packed totes. When items get picked out as customer orders are being fulfilled, Sparrow continually refills the totes. “When we think about how AI applies to the physical space in robotics, Sparrow was really the first place where we truly deployed foundation models that enabled us to deal with that high diversity of products,” Dresser told Ars. Such foundation models have helped the Amazon Robotics team to efficiently identify and classify those hundreds of millions of items to develop new robotic strategies for grasping them, Dresser explained. They also eliminated the need for manually coding every single item. More recently, the Amazon Robotics team has created a foundation model for robotic perception that can handle new, never-before-seen products that may end up in Amazon fulfillment centers. The team also developed a generalized action model compatible with different types of robotic arms that may use varying combinations of suction and finger grasping. “If you need to build ad hoc perception models for every such task and embodiment, that just doesn’t scale,” Kapil Katyal, a senior applied science manager at Amazon Robotics, said during a presentation attended by Ars at the Intelligent Robots & Systems conference held in Pittsburgh on September 29. “So one of the initiatives we took up in our team was to design a foundation model which could work for any new embodiment of robots that we deploy, and across all of the perception tasks that we need to perform over those,” Katyal said. Most of the robotic arms have relied primarily on vision. But Amazon’s newest robotic arm, Vulcan, is the first to combine vision with force feedback sensors capable of providing a sense of touch. This enables the robot to accurately pick out or stow away items within the fabric-covered inventory pods. “You need to be able to insert an item, create space, or pull something out,” Dresser said. “Those are really hard things to do with traditional non-touch-based systems today, like the suction-based systems that are part of Sparrow.” Vulcan initially began operating at Amazon fulfillment centers in Spokane, Washington, and Hamburg, Germany, starting in 2025. It is not widely deployed yet, but Dresser said the company expects to scale up use of this robotic arm in 2027. Each new robot has benefited Amazon’s human workforce, according to the company. For example, Vulcan focuses on picking and stowing items located in the top rows of inventory pods that would otherwise require a person to use a step-ladder, along with handling items located just above the floor. That enables employees to focus on picking and stowing items that are within more comfortable reach. But investigations by labor advocacy groups, journalists, and the US Congress have painted a less rosy picture of Amazon’s automation drive over the years. The Strategic Organizing Center (SOC), a coalition of trade unions headquartered in Washington, DC, published reports in May 2021 and April 2022 showing higher injury rates at Amazon’s robotic warehouses compared to non-robotic warehouses. That analysis drew on injury data reported by companies to the US government from 2017 through 2020. “Amazon likes to talk about its latest robots because it doesn’t want to talk about the tens of thousands of Amazon workers who are injured every year due to Amazon’s obsession with maximizing speed and minimizing costs,” Joan Moriarty, director of strategic research and Campaigns at SOC, said in an email to Ars. The organization also gathered nearly 1,000 responses from Amazon workers in an online survey conducted in February 2021. “Workers consistently report that the presence of robots increases the pace of work expected in Amazon facilities, forcing them to make fast, repetitive motions, substantially increasing the risk of injury,” Moriarty said. The US Senate Committee on Health, Education, Labor and Pensions (HELP) also conducted a 2024 investigation led by Sen. Bernie Sanders (I-Vt). The committee’s report noted how Amazon’s internal data also suggested a link between robotic productivity and increased pressure on human workers, leading to higher injury rates. Amazon has described these reports as flawed and being reliant on older data predating the deployment of its most modern robots, including the robotic arms that became operational over the past several years. The company also touted improvements in its overall workplace injury rates without detailing any potential differences between robotic and non-automated warehouses. “All of these claims ignore the fact that since 2019 our Global Recordable Incident Rate has improved 43 percent and our Global Lost Time Incident rate, which includes the most serious injuries that require employees to take time away from work, has improved 70 percent,” an Amazon spokesperson told Ars. “Our consistent year-over-year improvements have worked in parallel with our robotics growth across our operations network.” The most nuanced findings on Amazon’s automation drive may come from a research study published in the journal ILR Review in April 2025. That analysis showed Amazon’s robotic warehouses had a 40 percent decrease in severe injuries compared to legacy fulfillment centers—which suggests some benefits from having robots take on the most physically challenging and risky tasks. However, the same study also found that robotic fulfillment centers had a 77 percent increase in less severe injuries associated with physical strain from repetitive motions. “At least one of the mechanisms by which this appears to be manifesting is through the pace of work, in that pick rates for the robotic fulfillment centers are off the charts higher,” said Greenwood at George Mason University, who was one of the paper’s coauthors. A faster pace of work driven by partial automation could mean “the robot is dictating the pace of work” and spurring human employees to perform certain repetitive actions more quickly, Greenwood told Ars. “That appears to be what’s driving the strains and the sprains that we observe,” he said. The study drew in part on Amazon company data obtained by journalists at Reveal and The Center for Investigative Reporting, covering weekly data at more than 150 Amazon warehouses from 2016 through 2019. Although the data is older, Greenwood said the findings are still relevant to Amazon’s ongoing automation efforts. “I haven’t seen a tremendous amount of evidence to suggest that these dynamics are changing, inasmuch as you still have a tremendous amount of turnover in the warehousing context—and that’s not just Amazon,” Greenwood said. Greenwood and his colleagues also analyzed online comments posted on Reddit by Amazon fulfillment center employees. In those comments, Amazon employees described work at robotic fulfillment centers as being less physically stressful but also mentally exhausting because of the physical repetitiveness and faster pace associated with higher productivity expectations. An obvious question is whether automation efforts by Amazon and other companies will end up replacing some potential human hires with robots. For its part, Amazon has touted the robotics push as providing new job opportunities for human employees. Fulfillment centers with advanced robotics typically require 30 percent more employees working in reliability, maintenance, and engineering roles, and the new facilities for manufacturing additional robots will also supposedly bring more than 300 jobs each. “As we recognize that each of these robotics [developments] can change the nature of work, we’re providing opportunities for our employees to upskill and learn some of these things,” said Dresser at Amazon Robotics. “We’ve actually increased the number of robotic maintenance technicians that we have in the network by over 30 percent, and that is a much higher-skilled, higher-compensated job.” The company has also described its automation efforts as going hand in hand with a growing business that needs more human employees. “As we’ve deployed robots over the past decade, we’ve seen our operational footprint grow, creating hundreds of thousands of jobs as we expanded our network,” the Amazon spokesperson told Ars. However, there are apparently internal discussions within Amazon about robots potentially reducing the need to hire humans. In October 2025, The New York Times reported on internal strategy documents suggesting that the robotics push could allow the company to reduce projected hiring needs in the United States by more than 160,000 people by 2027 and 600,000 people by 2033. (An Amazon spokesperson responded at the time by saying the documents reflected the views of just one group inside the company.) Even as Amazon looks to build out its robotic fleet beyond the million-robot mark, discussions about the company’s workplace safety and working conditions are unlikely to go away. Although just a very small fraction of Amazon’s hundreds of thousands of employees have unionized in the United States, labor unions have “traditionally served a very good role in terms of improving worker safety,” Greenwood said. “Now, obviously, I understand that firms are not always excited to engage in that conversation, and I can’t say that would solve the problem here,” Greenwood told Ars. “But developing a better relationship between management and labor is usually the best path forward to try to figure out how to reduce these injury rates, which would have benefits for Amazon as well.” The first Amazon Labor Union that was organized among workers at a warehouse in Staten Island, New York, in April 2022, has been pushing what it describes as a Safety Bill of Rights that outlines changes it would like to see to make their workplace safer. The image that accompanies the document declares: “We are not robots.”
Ars Technica - All content
Review: 4-hour Musk documentary feels like two different films
2026-10-11

Review: 4-hour Musk documentary feels like two different films

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-11

Visit Original Website
Elon Musk is a very familiar figure to Ars readers, given his massive influence (for better and worse) on the technology sector over the last 20 years. Oscar-winning director Alex Gibney’s latest biographical documentary—simply titled Musk—covers a lot of well-known ground and challenges the carefully crafted public image of Musk as a visionary tech titan. Gibney’s documentary presents Musk as more of a Gilded Age confidence man, rejiggered for the 21st century: an investor cosplaying as an inventor whose real genius is pumping stocks and dazzling investors and the public alike with visions of a high-tech utopian future. The film is bound to push all the right outrage buttons, whether one is a Musk fan or a detractor—which makes it very much a product of the peculiar social media-driven era in which we live. Gibney has been making documentaries since the 1980s, covering such varied subjects as Jimi Hendrix, Hunter S. Thompson and gonzo journalism, WikiLeaks, Scientology, Steve Jobs, Elizabeth Holmes (the subject of another forthcoming documentary, You Can See Everything, featuring comedian Nathan Fielder), Boris Becker, The Sopranos creator David Chase, alleged Russian interference in the US 2016 presidential election, the opioid epidemic, and cyclist Lance Armstrong. He snagged his first Oscar nomination for 2005’s Enron: The Smartest Guys in the Room, and won the Oscar for Best Documentary Feature two years later for Taxi to the Dark Side. Tackling the subject of Elon Musk seems like a natural progression from those earlier works. Gibney has never been one to shy away from controversy, but even before Musk’s release, the film drew the ire of its subject and threats of a defamation lawsuit. There were even (as yet unfounded) rumors that Universal Pictures was getting cold feet about theatrically releasing the film in international markets. There’s certainly no shortage of material for Gibney to work with, and the film covers the most well-known aspects of Musk’s life: Tesla and the fatalities linked to its driver-assistance systems, SpaceX, Starlink, his purchase of Twitter, his shift from warning about the risks of AI to fully embracing it with Grok, his embrace of Trump, the creation of DOGE, his alleged drug use, and his 14 (possibly more) children. Those interviewed include ex-wife Justine Musk, Kara Swisher, Martin Eberhard, Geoffrey Hinton, former MAGA influencer Ashley St. Clair (who bore a child by Musk), Cory Doctorow, Adam Becker, and Sam Harris. David Byrne co-wrote and performed an original song for the soundtrack, “Let Me Sell You a Dream,” incorporating more of Musk’s words. Gibney is a gifted filmmaker and Musk is exceptionally well-done. But the documentary ends up feeling like two very different films. The first half focuses on the early entrepreneurial years, deftly incorporating archival footage with the usual talking heads. It reminds us of who Musk used to be—honestly, I miss that public version of Musk, even if it was carefully cultivated—and what little is known about his family background serves to humanize him a little. The second half focuses on Musk after his purchase of Twitter and embrace of Trump. The notoriously thin-skinned Musk has accused Gibney of making a “hit piece.” It’s not, but the tone in the second half is markedly different and certainly more adversarial, leaving little doubt as to where Gibney stands. (The director is a staunch critic of the so-called journalistic “view from nowhere,” and his documentaries typically have a strong point of view.) During a post-screening Q&A, Gibney revealed that he’d actually finished the documentary in 2024, but HBO asked him to keep going to document Musk’s “heel turn,” if you will. One assumes he recut the first half in the process, but perhaps that’s why Musk feels like two different documentary films smushed together. It’s also how the documentary ballooned to a nearly four-hour run time (with a 10-minute intermission). Gibney joked during the Q&A that the real question should be, how did he limit himself to just four hours when it could easily been stretched to six? In that case, perhaps it might have worked better as a six-episode documentary miniseries. Musk declined to be interviewed for the film, so the director created an AI version of Musk uttering verbatim public quotes as a stand-in for a real interview. I get why Gibney decided to do this and it’s conceptually apt: this is the AI future that Musk and his ilk are working so hard to bring about, popular opinion be damned. So sure, give him the AI avatar treatment. But the execution is lacking; it’s creepy uncanny valley stuff. The AI Musk pales in comparison with the archival footage of the actual Musk, which is far more interesting precisely because it’s real. Musk is a frequently compelling portrait of a complicated man, but that portrait is incomplete in some crucial ways. Gibney’s portrayal is as much tragic anti-hero as con artist or flim-flam man—an idealistic dreamer ultimately corrupted by wealth and power, or what Gibney calls “noble cause corruption.” The first half of the documentary made me mourn who Musk once was and wonder what kind of man he might have become had he made different choices at crucial points on his rise to the top. But the film sheds little light on why he ended up on such a dark path. Would Musk agreeing to be interviewed have helped? I am not so sure. This is a man, as the film makes clear, who really isn’t capable of self-reflection or facing hard truths, and he has the wherewithal to keep his bubble intact. As his wealth and influence have increased, so has his detachment from reality. Somehow it still hasn’t made him happy. Musk is now playing in select theaters in New York and Los Angeles. It will open broadly across the US on October 14, 2026. Credit: Bleecker Street Credit: Bleecker Street

Ars Technica - All content
One with the world? A new look at brains transformed by psychedelics.
2026-10-10

One with the world? A new look at brains transformed by psychedelics.

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-10

Visit Original Website
For about a decade, the mainstream understanding has been that psychedelics cause chaos in the brain. Brain networks—the stable patterns of communication that handle vision, attention, or our sense of self, and more—loosen and start talking to each other all at once. EEG traces get noisier and more complex. “Think of the networks of the brain as highways,” says Devon Stoliker, a neuroscientist at Monash University. “Under psychedelics, these highways break down into many different directions.” In a recent Nature study, Stoliker’s team designed an experiment to test whether these directions are truly chaotic and random. Researchers gathered 62 people who had never taken a psychedelic before, gave them 19 milligrams of psilocybin, a common psychedelic found in certain fungi, and scanned their brains. Then, they used AI to analyze the drugged and sober brain scans. What emerged was not exactly chaos. Stoliker thinks chaos is not a satisfactory explanation for psychedelics’ effect on the brain. “It never really explained why an individual would have a meaningful experience, why they might have insight, why they might experience clarity, and why these might translate into positive psychological changes,” Stoliker says. To investigate if there are other explanations, he and his colleagues designed a study called PsiConnect. Volunteers went through the same four-part sequence twice, once sober and once on psilocybin. The sequence included putting participants in four different settings or contexts: eight minutes of lying at rest, a guided meditation, an 11-minute curated music playlist, and finally six minutes of eyes-open video of clouds moving across a sky. Each participant did all four inside an MRI scanner about 80 minutes after dosing, and again on EEG about 150 minutes after. There were no cognitive tasks researchers routinely go for in brain studies, but there was a purpose behind this omission. “We wanted ecological validity,” Stoliker says. “We wanted to know what the brain was like when somebody was having an authentic, uninterrupted psychedelic experience. If somebody took a psychedelic in a therapeutic setting, you wouldn’t have them completing tasks.” Stoliker also points out that there’s evidence that giving someone a task mid-trip pulls them out of the state—a phenomenon researchers sometimes call grounding. “Once you introduce these sorts of tasks, you’re actually interrupting the very phenomena you are seeking to measure.” And it worked. Half of the participants ranked their session among the most meaningful experiences of their lives, and 24 put it in their personal top five. Stoliker and his colleagues started analyzing their brain scan data to find out why. Measuring global functional connectivity—how much influence each patch of cortex exerts over the rest—the team found that, when participants had their eyes closed, sensory regions lost sway while associative regions gained. “It seems like the brain’s ability to construct reality, or imagination, or our associations, our beliefs, our sense of self—these faculties had more dominance over sensory areas,” Stoliker says, stressing that the interpretation is a hypothetical. “This could help explain why people have meaningful, complex imagery experiences, why they have mystical experiences with imagery that is personally relevant to them.” Another observation the team made was that connections within each brain network weakened, connections between networks strengthened. The brain’s modularity, a parameter that describes how cleanly neurons stay sorted into specialist teams, dropped across all four parts of the experimental sequence. What’s more, researchers found that, when sober, activity in a brain with its eyes shut looked very different from a brain watching a movie. Under psilocybin, that difference nearly evaporated. In the visual network, the gap between eyes-open and eyes-closed connectivity shrank by 85 percent. The results from the EEG session independently confirmed that, with alpha-band activity (normally a marker of the brain gating visual input) reduced by nearly half. “When somebody takes a psychedelic and they’re able to close their eyes and see complex imagery, there seems to be less boundary between the internal and external world than we ordinarily experience,” Stoliker says. Regardless of these details, the results rather accurately reproduced the chaos in the brain so many researchers claimed psychedelics caused. This chaos, though, turned into order when the team processed their data in a slightly unorthodox way. Scientists usually do two acts of averaging in a standard brain imaging study. The first is averaging over time. An eight-minute brain scan comprises a few hundred successive images of the whole brain. To understand how two specific regions work together, researchers conventionally average all these images down to a single number describing how well the two regions’ activity matched up across the whole eight minutes. The problem with this analysis is that it runs the risk of missing ordered structures that appear for a brief period and then disappear into chaos. The second analysis is averaging over people. Having produced one such number per participant, the standard approach then pools all of them into a group average, on the assumption that individual differences are noise that will be canceled out. Stoliker’s study was focused on individual experiences, so he wanted to avoid that. Instead, the team fed the moment-by-moment activity of 332 brain regions into CEBRA, a machine-learning tool that compressed the data down to its essential structure while preserving the order of events. For each individual, it produced a trajectory—a path traced through a three-dimensional space, one point for each moment of the scan. “When they’re not under psilocybin, the brain activity is less differentiated by context,” Stoliker says. “But under psilocybin, we see that the activity becomes organized across time and more clearly differentiated by context.” The trajectories separated into four distinct clusters corresponding to rest, meditation, music, and the movie. A classifier could read a moment of brain activity and say which one the person was in. It turned out the performance of that classifier scaled with how profound the participants described their experience as being. To find out which parts of the brain were doing the work, the team replaced one network’s psilocybin activity with its sober version and checked how badly the classifier degraded. The default mode network and the visual network each accounted for over 20 percent of the effect. “The default mode network is strongly associated with the sense of self: daydreaming, mind wandering, thinking about yourself. It’s often been called the narrative self,” Stoliker explains. “On the other end of this gradient, more externally oriented, is the visual system.” Under psilocybin, the two became less differentiated, which the team thinks explains why participants reported a subjective experience of the self and the external world becoming less separate, a state Stoliker calls “embeddedness.” “Theoretically, we could suggest that psychedelics are temporarily altering the organization of brain networks that ordinarily help maintain this separation that we experience,” he says. “It really challenges the idea of whether the internal world and external world are separate and highlights the idea that the brain may be responsible for constructing both.” The day after the session, participants rated whether various things had shifted, like sense of connection to themselves, to others, to nature, along with peace, acceptance, and creativity, all on a scale running from –100 to 100. “For the vast majority of our participants, we found they had positive psychological changes the next day,” Stoliker says. The data from the brain reading classifier the team used for discriminating among meditation, rest, the movie, and the music tracked with the size of that shift. In the future, Stoliker hopes to use this for therapeutic purposes. If context isn’t just mood-setting but is mechanically shaping brain organization, Stoliker argues, then the room, the playlist, and the instructions start to look like clinical variables a clinician could deliberately tune to improve therapeutic outcomes. But there are details the team must work out first. As of now, the researchers admit that when two people report identical “embeddedness” scores, they can potentially have very different subjective experiences. Also, all the volunteers in Stoliker’s study were healthy individuals, not patients suffering from psychological or psychiatric disorders that potential therapies using psychedelics could be aimed at. Finally, we still don’t know how exactly variables like music or visuals should be tuned. “That’s a future avenue where we need more research to determine exactly what conditions should be optimized, and for who. It’s quite possible we would want to take different approaches for each individual, or depending on what their diagnosis is,” Stoliker says. “Still, finding this level of organization hidden underneath that disorder and chaos has some really good explanatory value.” Nature, 2026. DOI: 10.1038/s41586-026-10910-z
Fortune | FORTUNE
Ella Langley spent a record 24 weeks at No. 1. Most U.S. music fans didn’t know her name—signaling a major cultural shift
2026-10-10

Ella Langley spent a record 24 weeks at No. 1. Most U.S. music fans didn’t know her name—signaling a major cultural shift

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-10

Visit Original Website
The internet can’t seem to get over one question: How did a country song that so many people say they’ve never heard, by a singer they’ve never heard of either, set the record for the most weeks at No. 1 in the Billboard Hot 100’s 68-year history? Ella Langley’s “Choosin’ Texas” logged 24 weeks atop the chart, passing the 22-week record held by Mariah Carey’s “All I Want for Christmas Is You,” the Christmas staple you can’t escape hearing every winter. The run finally ended on the chart dated Oct. 10, when Taylor Swift’s “Patient Zero” debuted at No. 1. Carey’s song, which tends to return to No. 1 each December, would need three more weeks at the top to reclaim the record. Just 24% of U.S. music listeners knew of Ella Langley in the second quarter of 2026, according to entertainment data and research company Luminate, which shared the figures with Fortune. That was up from 13% in the fourth quarter of 2025 and 17% in the first quarter of this year. Her recognition was growing, but it still reached fewer than one in four music listeners. A No. 1 hit, apparently, can still leave people asking, “Who?” So what happened to monoculture? For those unfamiliar with the term, monoculture is a shared set of cultural references: the songs, shows, and movies you could assume other people knew, whether they liked them or not. (Lately, the word has also taken on a second, almost opposite meaning, as critics use it to describe how algorithms and AI flatten culture into sameness.) It was easier to share references when so much of what people watched and heard came through a few television networks, radio stations, and MTV. Industry gatekeepers picked what reached a mass audience. You didn’t have to be a fan to know what everyone was talking about. That shared audience was never everyone, and it began fragmenting before TikTok. But personalized feeds take the division further: What reaches you increasingly depends on what the system thinks you, specifically, will want to see. Take TikTok, which touts the personalization of its For You feed. As the company explains, “there’s no one For You feed.” Has this ever happened to you? You open social media, watch one Olivia Rodrigo concert clip, and suddenly it feels like every fifth video is another one. TikTok says it takes cues from what you like and share, whom you follow, and whether you watch a video to the end. It also looks at the videos themselves: their captions, sounds, and hashtags. Even your language preference, country setting, and device type factor in, though TikTok says those carry less weight. All of that helps the system decide what deserves another few seconds of your attention. TikTok says it also works to keep feeds varied, for example by generally not showing two videos in a row that use the same sound or come from the same creator. In the U.S., that system is now overseen by the Oracle-backed joint venture that took over TikTok’s American operations in January, which has said it will retrain the recommendation algorithm on U.S. user data. Keep watching, and the algorithm may keep serving up more of the same. Researchers have observed that feedback loop in action. A study published in EPJ Data Science in February tested TikTok’s For You feed using automated accounts programmed with different interests. It found that TikTok increasingly recommended videos matching those interests, with that reinforcement typically kicking in within the first 200 videos watched. The feeds kept some variety, but the more strongly an account’s interests were amplified, the fewer different hashtags it tended to encounter. (The researchers ran their experiments in 2024, before TikTok’s U.S. ownership change.) That helps explain why you think something is taking over the internet, while Joe next door gets a completely different feed and wonders what you’re talking about. You can also help narrow the selection yourself. Platforms like TikTok offer tools, such as its Manage Topics setting, that let you tell the system which topics you want to see more or less of. The clues used to personalize your feed can even come from outside the app. Meta announced in June that it would use information other businesses already share with it, like purchases people make on other websites, to personalize users’ feeds and AI responses, not just ads. The company’s own example: Buy a tent online, and you might start seeing more camping Reels. Users can manage this through a setting called “activity from other businesses.” What you watch, what you buy—it can all feed into the calculation, shaping your own little corner of the internet. One just for you. Not everyone is a fan. New Yorker writer Kyle Chayka has a few complaints about that convenience—a whole book’s worth, in fact: Filterworld: How Algorithms Flattened Culture. “These digital platforms and feeds, they kind of promise a great communal experience … but I think they’re actually kind of atomizing our experiences, because we can never tell what other people are seeing in their own feeds,” he told NPR’s Fresh Air in 2024. That, he argued, takes some of the joy out of art and makes it harder for people to get excited about the same things. Smarter algorithms may leave us in separate bubbles. But if something doesn’t interest you, do you really want it in your feed just for a shared cultural experience? Then again, if it never reaches you, how would you know whether you’d like it? © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. All Rights Reserved. Ship captains and crews transiting the Strait of Hormuz make so much danger pay that they’re ‘almost being viewed as mercenaries’
2026-10-10

Ship captains and crews transiting the Strait of Hormuz make so much danger pay that they’re ‘almost being viewed as mercenaries’

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-10

Visit Original Website
The high-risk, high-reward business of moving oil through the Strait of Hormuz has gotten riskier and more rewarding. Despite steady attacks from Iran, oil tankers and other commercial vessels have continued flowing through the narrow waterway. With protection from the U.S. military and help from pipelines bypassing the strait, Persian Gulf oil flows returned to prewar levels. But seeking to reassert its leverage over the global oil chokepoint, Iran has stepped up drone and missile strikes on ships recently, including those outside the immediate vicinity of Hormuz. While traffic has dipped in response, ships continue to cross, with many making shuttle runs in and out of the Gulf to unload oil cargoes via ship-to-ship transfers. Then other vessels deliver the barrels to customers. For those onboard the ships willing to tolerate the risks in the Gulf, a hefty payday awaits. Tanker captains can earn $100,000 a month for transiting the strait, plus a $50,000 bonus for each trip, sources told the Financial Times. That “danger money” is up from regular pay of about $15,000 a month and is necessary for shipowners to keep their fleets crewed. Meanwhile, sailors typically make at little as $1,500 a month. But trips through Hormuz can bump up earnings by at least four to six times their normal rates, the report said. Other hotspots also offer hazard enhancements, albeit less than what’s available for Hormuz trips. According to the FT, captains and sailors get double pay in the southern Red Sea, where Houthis have been attacking Saudi ships, and in the Gulf of Oman, where many ship-to-ship transfers take place after Hormuz shuttle runs. Because many ships and their crews are dedicated to the shuttle runs, they are in constant peril—allowing them to stack up all the bonuses and danger money for each trip. A source told the FT that those brave enough to stomach the nearly constant threat of attacks are “almost being viewed as mercenaries,” though some unwilling crew members are reportedly being pressured to stay onboard. Since the Iran war started on Feb. 28, at least 93 ships have been hit, and 24 sailors have been killed, according to the International Maritime Organization. In addition to the hazard pay for crews, shipowners are also paying for insurance, and the war risk can command 6% to 10% of a ship’s value—translating to up to $20 million for a supertanker sailing through the Gulf. All the added costs have made shipping expensive too. Freight rates for cargoes crossing the Strait of Hormuz hit a record high of $1.3 million per day, up from last year’s daily rate of $20,000 to $50,000. All the ships needed to get oil out of the Gulf have also contributed to a global tanker shortage that’s spiked freight rates around the world. Shipbroker Gibson pointed out that hiring a tanker to travel from the U.S. to China costs about $80 million, more than the $74 million price tag for a standard SpaceX Falcon 9 launch. Brokerage SSY estimated that rates are now the highest since the advent of the supertanker in the 1960s, even after adjusting for inflation. Shipping costs have become so extreme that oil producers and even commodities traders are looking to own their own tankers to control the expenses. But if freight costs get too high, they could erase the margins that can be earned from oil and refined fuels. For instance, European refiner Repsol saw its margin drop from $36 per barrel in the third quarter to $15 in October, according to analysts at RBC. Further reduction in margins could eventually force refiners to reduce how much crude they process. The hazard premium could get worse in the Persian Gulf. Analysts have warned the Iranian regime, facing an existential threat to its rule, could reescalate the war and take back its grip on the oil trade. Esfandyar Batmanghelidj, founder and CEO of the Bourse & Bazaar Foundation think tank, pointed out recently that Iran can still destroy regional oil infrastructure, such as drilling and refining capacity, and can escalate if President Donald Trump rejects diplomatic off-ramps. “Iran did not wage a scorched earth campaign—but it may yet do so if the current situation persists for too long,” he posted on X late last month. “That is what everyone who actually follows this region and its dynamics is worried about.” Jason Ma is the weekend editor at Fortune, where he covers markets, the economy, finance, and housing. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. All Rights Reserved. Jeff Bezos says he gets why socialism is surging—but politicians blaming ‘greedy landlords’ have housing all wrong
2026-10-12

Jeff Bezos says he gets why socialism is surging—but politicians blaming ‘greedy landlords’ have housing all wrong

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-12

Visit Original Website
Jeff Bezos says he understands why socialism is gaining traction in America. The affordability crisis is real, especially when it comes to housing. But the Amazon and Blue Origin founder argues that some politicians are blaming the wrong culprit. In an interview with Fox News’ Bret Baier that aired Wednesday on Special Report, Bezos said government intervention has often been counterproductive in housing, while some politicians point fingers at “greedy landlords” instead of addressing what he called a basic supply-and-demand problem. “It’s econ 101,” he said. In the same interview, Bezos predicted AI could usher in a three-day workweek, as Fortune reported. He has also argued that raising taxes on billionaires won’t fix affordability. Americans are growing more receptive to socialism, as their views of Big Business sour. Capitalism still has broader appeal, with 55% of Americans viewing it positively, according to a Gallup poll released in September. But a record 43% now view socialism positively, the highest share since Gallup began tracking the question in 2010. Meanwhile, positive views of Big Business have fallen from 49% in 2010 to just 35%. The generational divide is stark. Among Americans ages 18 to 34, 57% view socialism positively, compared with 43% who view capitalism favorably. For Americans 55 and older, the picture is reversed: 65% view capitalism positively, versus 34% for socialism. In a separate Gallup survey, 26% of Americans cited a stronger safety net and more affordable essentials, including housing, health care, and education, as benefits of socialism. Still, 45% either said nothing was good about socialism or couldn’t name any of its benefits. Even Vice President JD Vance warned in July that young Americans priced out of homeownership could be drawn to socialism, as Fortune previously reported. At the end of 2025, a household needed $120,800 a year to afford payments on a median-price U.S. home, up from $68,700 five years earlier, according to Harvard University’s Joint Center for Housing Studies. Only 32% of U.S. households met that income threshold, including just 16% of renter households. An earlier report from the same Harvard center found that rising homebuying costs between 2021 and 2024 effectively priced out about 8 million renter households that previously earned enough to afford a median-price home. Most were between age 25 and 44, in their prime homebuying years. First-time buyers made up just 21% of home purchases in the National Association of Realtors’ latest annual survey, a record low, and Zillow’s CEO doesn’t expect that to change soon. Bezos took aim at “government programs that subsidize demand and constrain supply.” He pointed to Austin, where he said policies have made it easier to build homes and housing prices have fallen. “Some politicians are governing correctly on this issue,” he said. Austin has numbers to back at least part of his argument. The city added 120,000 housing units between 2015 and 2024, while median monthly rent fell from $1,546 in December 2021 to $1,296 in January 2026, according to the Pew Charitable Trusts. In the broader Austin metro area, the median home listing price fell 9.8% year over year to $446,450 in September, according to Realtor.com. But Austin isn’t the only place where prices are falling. Nationally, the median listing price fell 1.4% year over year in September, the 11th straight month of annual declines, according to Realtor.com’s September housing report. A record share of sellers for this time of year have also been cutting asking prices, as Fortune previously reported, including about 26% of listings in the Austin metro area. The broader slowdown makes it harder to pin Austin’s price drop on new construction alone. Austin’s housing push involved more than one policy. Starting in 2015, the city loosened zoning rules to allow more apartments, and in 2023 it scrapped minimum parking requirements citywide. Voters approved $250 million in bonds in 2018 to help build and repair affordable housing, followed by another $350 million in 2022. Yet despite the construction boom and falling rents, Pew noted that housing gaps remain even larger for lower-income residents, especially those hoping to buy a home. With apartment completions slowing, Pew also warned that rents could start rising again. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. All Rights Reserved. Jeff Bezos says people have been ‘incorrectly’ taught that success is a villain: ‘You can be a billionaire if you started a hamburger stand,’ he slams
2026-10-12

Jeff Bezos says people have been ‘incorrectly’ taught that success is a villain: ‘You can be a billionaire if you started a hamburger stand,’ he slams

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-12

Visit Original Website
Jeff Bezos has taken Amazon from an online bookstore he founded in his garage in 1995—to the No. 1 company on the Fortune 500. His own net worth has soared, too, with a net worth of $372 billion making him the second-richest person in the world. However, the 62-year-old argues that people who view extreme wealth like his own as a bad thing have been taught to think about success the wrong way. “I think we live in an environment where some people have been taught, I think incorrectly, but taught, that this whole kind of oppressor/oppressed framework, and success is villainy,” Bezos recently told Fox News. “Success is not villainy. You can be a billionaire if you started a hamburger stand.” Bezos pointed to how someone could start with a simple food stand with 10 or so workers, grow it into a business with thousands of locations and employees, and eventually become a billionaire. “This is how it works,” he said. “There’s nothing wrong with making unbelievably good chicken that people love. And it doesn’t make him a bad person.” Many people, particularly younger generations, may disagree with Bezos’s assessment. Some 18% of Americans view being extremely rich as “morally wrong,” a figure that nearly doubles to about 33% among Gen Z adults aged 18 and 29, according to a 2025 Pew Research study. That skepticism comes as billionaire wealth continues to soar. In 2025, the fortunes of the world’s billionaires reached a record $18.3 trillion, according to Oxfam. Meanwhile, the American Dream of buying a home has increasingly felt out of reach for many households, particularly younger Americans struggling with housing costs. Bezos, for his part, acknowledged that the country races real affordability problems. But he argued that voters should be wary of politicians who focus on assigning blame rather than addressing the underlying problems. “You can’t pretend there’s no problems,” he said. “Young people look at the cost of housing and think, I have to live two hours out of town. I can’t live close to my job.” It’s the same problem-solving approach he expects from executives at his own companies. “If there’s somebody at Amazon or Blue Origin or Prometheus, an executive at one of my companies, instead of working on a root cause and a real fix, all they did is point fingers and blame people, we would exit them quickly,” Bezos said. “They wouldn’t be here long.” Earlier this year, Bezos floated a proposal to ease the tax burden on lower-income Americans, suggesting that the bottom half of U.S. income earners—roughly 76 million households—should pay no federal income taxes. “The bottom half of income earners in this country pay only 3% of the taxes,” Bezos told CNBC in May. “I think it should be zero.” The 62-year-old billionaire pointed to a hypothetical health care worker in Queens who makes $75,000 a year—but pays more than $1,000 a month in taxes. “To me, it’s kind of absurd that we’re doing this,” Bezos said. “We shouldn’t be asking this nurse in Queens to send money to Washington. They should be sending her an apology. It really makes no sense.” Bezos isn’t alone in pushing back against the idea that success—and the billions of dollars that can come with it—should be viewed as inherently bad. Former Starbucks CEO Howard Schultz, who has about $3.4 billion to his name, has also argued that his fortune reflects years of hard work rather than an unfair advantage. “This moniker ‘billionaire,’ let’s get at that okay?” Schultz said during a heated U.S. Senate committee hearing in 2023. “I grew up in federally subsidized housing, my parents never owned a home, I came from nothing. I thought my entire life was based on the achievement of the American dream. Yes I have billions of dollars, I earned it. No one gave it to me.” JPMorgan Chase CEO Jamie Dimon, who has a net worth of about $3 billion, has similarly acknowledged concerns about wealth inequality while pushing back against the idea that wealthy people should be vilified simply for their success. Dimon told 60 Minutes in 2019: “I think you should vilify Nazis, but you shouldn’t vilify people who’ve worked hard to accomplish things.” Preston Fore is a reporter on Fortune's Success team. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. The IEA wants to empty its oil reserves faster. A federal watchdog says the U.S. isn’t ready for what comes next
2026-10-12

The IEA wants to empty its oil reserves faster. A federal watchdog says the U.S. isn’t ready for what comes next

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-12

Visit Original Website
Governments are tapping their emergency oil faster than ever, and the room to keep doing so is shrinking. On Oct. 7, member governments of the International Energy Agency backed speeding up the oil stock releases they pledged under the agency’s March 2026 Collective Action, aiming to finish them as soon as possible. They also agreed to prioritize diesel, given how tight that market has become. According to Reuters, JPMorgan analysts read the move as an acceleration of barrels already promised, not a fresh intervention. The March action was historic on its own. In response to the Iran war and the disruption of tanker traffic through the Strait of Hormuz, IEA members agreed to make 400 million barrels of oil available to the market. It was only the sixth collective action since the agency’s founding in 1974, and by far the largest. Seven months later, the buffer is visibly thinner. IEA governments still hold roughly 1.1 billion barrels of publicly held emergency stocks, including more than 200 million barrels of diesel, the agency says. In March, that figure was more than 1.2 billion barrels. Including company inventories, Saudi Aramco CEO Amin Nasser estimated this week that more than 1 billion barrels have been pulled from stockpiles worldwide. The IEA’s move followed an Oct. 2 videoconference of G7 leaders convened by French President Emmanuel Macron. In a joint statement, leaders said they had agreed on coordinated measures to “stabilize immediate energy supplies, shield households and businesses from price shocks.” The core of the plan is a coordinated release of 100 million barrels through the IEA over four months, with a large share of diesel front-loaded into the first 20 days. The G7 also pledged to stagger refinery maintenance so plants don’t go offline at the same time. Members said they would push refiners to run harder where feasible and encourage countries with significant refining capacity to boost diesel output. They also reaffirmed that they would not restrict energy exports among themselves. Diesel is the pressure point. Damage to refineries and disrupted shipping from the wars in Iran and Ukraine have pushed diesel to record highs in many markets, feeding directly into trucking, farming, and freight costs. On Sunday, IEA executive director Fatih Birol wrote to member governments ahead of the agency’s Oct. 15 governing board meeting. He urged all of them, including those that have already completed their pledged releases, to consider whether more diesel needs to come out of storage. In the U.S., the strain is showing in the Strategic Petroleum Reserve. The Trump administration began a 172-million-barrel emergency release in March as its share of the IEA effort. The SPR has since fallen to about 284 million barrels, its lowest level since 1982, and is on track to drop to roughly 243 million once the final barrels are delivered. Energy Secretary Chris Wright has warned that drawing the reserve down too far could damage the salt caverns where the oil is stored. That is a sharp reversal from the administration’s earlier pledge to refill the SPR “right to the top.” A report from the Government Accountability Office, published in May and released publicly in June, suggests the problems run deeper than inventory levels. GAO found that DOE has released more than 500 million barrels of crude from the SPR since 1985, more than half for emergencies such as wars. Nearly 70% of all releases occurred between 2014 and 2025. Before this year, the largest drawdown on record was the 180-million-barrel emergency sale in 2022 after Russia invaded Ukraine. That pace has collided with aging infrastructure. GAO found that investment in the reserve isn’t keeping up with its needs, leaving a growing maintenance backlog. DOE itself has warned that aging equipment will increasingly limit the SPR’s ability to carry out fill or drawdown orders. The bigger gap, GAO argues, is strategic. DOE hasn’t completed a long-term review of the SPR since 2016, and Congress has never set a target size. “Without an updated long-term plan or target size for the SPR, DOE and Congress are making operational and investment decisions amidst uncertainty about what the SPR is capable of at present, what it should be capable of in the future to meet changing U.S. needs and obligations, and what investments and other actions may be required to bridge any gaps,” the report states. GAO urged Congress to require DOE to complete long-term SPR plans on a set timeline. It also recommended that DOE reassess the technical criteria governing how fast the reserve can be drawn down and refilled. DOE concurred with the recommendations. Under IEA rules, member countries must hold oil stocks equivalent to at least 90 days of net imports. Net exporters are exempt. Governments have wide latitude in how they meet that obligation, using some mix of government-held reserves, dedicated stockholding agencies, and stocks that industry is required to keep. The harder question is how and when those stocks get rebuilt. The G7 asked the IEA to deliver a follow-up report within 20 days with recommendations to improve future responses, including how to replenish stocks. IEA governments will review the situation again at the Oct. 15 governing board meeting. Refilling won’t be cheap or quick. Analysts have noted that governments rebuilding their reserves after the war, possibly to levels above where they stood before it, would create a new layer of oil demand on top of normal consumption. That could keep prices elevated long after the shooting stops. For now, governments are leaning on a cushion they’ve already worn thin. As the IEA put it, it stands ready to release more “if and when required.” The question is how much is left to give. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. All Rights Reserved.
