Palantir’s billionaire CEO says only two kinds of people will succeed in the AI era: trade workers—‘or you’re neurodivergent’
2026-10-10

Palantir’s billionaire CEO says only two kinds of people will succeed in the AI era: trade workers—‘or you’re neurodivergent’

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-10

Visit Original Website
From Gen Z to baby boomers, workers across industries are on the hunt for ways to future-proof their careers as artificial intelligence threatens to upend the labor market. Palantir CEO Alex Karp is offering a starkly simple view of who will come out ahead. “There are basically two ways to know you have a future,” the 59-year-old billionaire said on TBPN in March. “One, you have some vocational training. Or two, you’re neurodivergent.” Karp’s first category reflects a growing consensus: Skilled trades professionals—from electricians to plumbers—are difficult to automate and are increasingly in demand as Big Tech companies build out massive data centers and the U.S. faces existing labor shortages. The second category is more personal. Karp has long spoken about living with dyslexia, the learning disability that can affect reading, writing, and information processing. More broadly, neurodivergence can include conditions such as ADHD and autism. For Karp, that cognitive difference can be an advantage in an AI-driven world—less because of the diagnosis itself and more because of the mindset it can foster. Success, he argued, will favor people who think differently and take risks, or in his words, be “more of an artist, look at things from a different direction, be able to build something unique.” One-fifth of sales organizations within Fortune 500 companies are expected to actively recruit neurodivergent talent to improve business performance by 2027, according to a Gartner study. While being neurodivergent is not a requirement to land a job at Palantir, the company has made it clear it sees such candidates as a strategic advantage. It offers a dedicated Neurodivergent Fellowship, aimed at recruiting talent that may think differently from traditional hires. “Neurodivergent individuals will play a disproportionate role in shaping the future of America and the West,” the job posting stated. “They see past performative ideologies and perceive beauty in the world that still exists—which technology and art can expose.” The emphasis reflects Karp’s broader skepticism of traditional career pathways. Despite holding three degrees—including a JD from Stanford and a PhD in philosophy from Goethe University in Germany—Karp has been blunt about the limits of higher education in an AI-driven economy. “[AI] will destroy humanities jobs,” Karp said at the World Economic Forum’s annual meeting in Davos, Switzerland, in early 2026. “You went to an elite school, and you studied philosophy—I’ll use myself as an example. Hopefully, you have some other skill. That one is going to be hard to market.” Palantir has also launched a separate program—the Meritocracy Fellowship—designed specifically for high school graduates not enrolled in college. The program’s first cohort required Ivy League–level test scores to qualify and attracted over 500 applicants. The 22 admitted students were a mix of those who felt attending college wasn’t compelling, or didn’t get into their dream schools, according to the Wall Street Journal. The fellowship’s fall 2026 cohort offered participants $5,400 a month as a stipend and pitched itself with a clear message: “Skip the debt. Reclaim years of your life. Earn the Palantir degree.” Top performers can even receive full-time offers at the company. As traditional entry-level roles dry up for Gen Z graduates, many young people are coming to a similar conclusion as Karp: A college degree alone is no longer a guaranteed path to success. Still, some tech leaders argue that higher education is far from obsolete—and that liberal arts in particular may become more valuable in the age of AI. Jaime Teevan, Microsoft’s chief scientist, believes the next generation will benefit from studying disciplines that emphasize how to think, not just what to do. “Metacognitive skills will be very important—flexibility, adaptability, experimentation, thinking critically, being able to challenge things. Developing critical-thinking skills requires friction, doing things that are hard, doing deep thinking,” she told the Wall Street Journal. “For that, a traditional liberal arts education is really important.” In direct contrast to Karp, Daniela Amodei, cofounder of AI firm Anthropic, said studying the humanities will be “more important than ever.” “The things that make us human will become much more important instead of much less important,” she told ABC News in February. “And what I mean by that is when we look to hire people at Anthropic today, we look for people who are great communicators, who have excellent EQ and people skills, who are kind and compassionate and curious and want to help other people.” A version of this story was published on Fortune.com on March 24, 2026. OpenAI to create 200 Ireland-based roles at new EU headquarters

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-12

New roles within the Applied AI Lab will include forward deployed engineers, solutions and privacy engineers, as well as sales and user operations staff. OpenAI has today (12 October) announced that it intends to create 200 new jobs in Ireland. The successful candidates will join the workforce at its new Dublin-based Applied AI Lab, based at the company’s EU headquarters. The 200 roles will be in addition to the previously announced 250 new jobs announced in July of this year, bringing the company’s total number of new jobs in Ireland this year to 450 roles. Currently OpenAI employs around 120 people in Ireland and the organisation is looking to add professionals who can support the new Applied AI Lab, including forward deployed engineers that will work directly with customers to build and deploy AI applications, as well as solutions and privacy engineers. Hiring will also take place in other areas such as sales and user operations. The organisation also announced plans to extend its ‘OpenAI for Ireland’ programme for start-ups, SMEs and builders. A new ‘Founder Cafe’ initiative, in partnership with Dogpatch Labs, aims to improve advanced AI skills among 150 high-potential founders from across Ireland, in areas such as ChatGPT, Codex and API, with the goal of innovating faster and helping to bring business ideas to life. Commenting on the announcement, Taoiseach Micheál Martin, TD said: “As Ireland hosts the International AI Summit this week as a flagship initiative of our EU Presidency, this further investment by OpenAI is a vote of confidence in the strength of our people, economy and ecosystem, which sets Ireland apart. “These factors combine to make Ireland a leading destination for applied AI research, innovation and commercialisation and this is a position the Government will build upon in the years ahead.” Sarah Friar, the CFO of OpenAI said: “Since we opened our EU headquarters in Dublin three years ago, Ireland has been critical to our success in Europe. I’m delighted we’re deepening our investment here, creating 200 more jobs and helping turn advances in frontier AI into local opportunity. “Through our new Applied AI Lab, expansion of our partnership with Dogpatch Labs and wider roll-out of the SME Academy, we want to help Irish businesses grow, support founders in turning ideas into thriving companies and equip more people across the country to benefit from AI.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. How can habits and technical skill ground people beginning their careers?
2026-10-12

How can habits and technical skill ground people beginning their careers?

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-12

Visit Original Website
BMS’ Jack Kneafsey offers his advice on how young people and career starters can maximise the intern experience. Jack Kneafsey, a senior manager for sterile drug product operations and co-lead of the BMS Intern Programme, joined Bristol Myers Squibb (BMS) in January of 2024, at a time when the team was relatively small. He told SiliconRepublic.com: “When the opportunity arose to co-lead the BMS intern programme as part of the University Relations team, I saw it as a valuable way to connect with colleagues across the campus and support a ‘one campus’ approach to developing early-career talent, including future talent who may contribute to our evolving site operations over time.” He explained that one of the most rewarding aspects of his role is when there is tangible evidence of an intern’s progress and professional development, particularly when they have the opportunity to explain the projects they have worked on to colleagues, senior leaders and university representatives. He said: “It gives our colleagues the chance to see first-hand the skills and confidence the interns develop through meaningful project work. It’s also a valuable opportunity to engage with university representatives and students, gather feedback on the programme experience and identify practical ways to continue strengthening future internship programmes.” It is very important. The habits, confidence and technical grounding people build in their first professional experience can shape how they approach their early career. That is why the BMS intern programme is structured around the 70:20:10 model, a learning and development approach that combines practical experience, support from others and formal training. In practice, this combines day-to-day experience through project work and cross-functional collaboration, as well as feedback and mentorship from hiring managers, the intern team and graduate mentors and formal learning opportunities such as presentation workshops, CV and interview preparation and Lean Six Sigma training. The internship programme is an important part of how we build early-career capability and strengthen our future talent pipeline. Interns who complete the programme gain hands-on industry experience, exposure to live projects and opportunities to develop their professional judgement in a real operational environment. That experience can help them build confidence and capability as they progress in their studies and consider future career opportunities. While outcomes can vary from year to year, the programme contributes to our graduate and early-career hiring pipeline. For BMS, this reinforces the value of investing in meaningful experiences, structured support and long-term development for early-career talent. For us, it comes down to structure and intention. For many interns this is their first professional role, so we build a framework around that transition. Most join in January with a dedicated induction to BMS, the campus and the University Relations team, followed by coffee mornings to get to know their peers and hiring managers. Every intern is paired with a graduate buddy for the questions they might not want to raise with a manager and managers themselves receive formal training on setting interns up for success, with any concerns fed back to our team so we can step in. That support continues throughout the year, from our ‘Day in the Life’ series, which encourages interns to present to their peers and build communication confidence, to social and networking moments such as pizza evenings and poster presentations. The aim is to create an environment where students feel comfortable asking questions, engaging with colleagues across the business and building relationships with peers, managers and senior leaders, while also developing communication, networking and technical skills that support early-career growth. Come in with a curious mindset. Ask questions and be comfortable doing so. It can feel intimidating at first, but we welcome that engagement. One of the most valuable things interns can gain from an internship in biopharma is an appreciation of the range of career paths available, so they should take opportunities to learn about different areas of the business. Our ‘Day in the Life’ series is one way to understand what different departments do day to day, and where appropriate, interns can speak to peers and managers about opportunities to learn more. I would also encourage every intern to step away from the desk and learn more about the wider Cruiserath Campus when they can. Seeing different parts of the site first-hand gives interns a broader appreciation for the scale, collaboration and expertise involved in our work. Most importantly, they should use their time here to build their CV, learn to talk confidently about their experiences and seek feedback from their hiring manager. Having the internship is one thing, being able to clearly articulate the experience is what helps them present themselves confidently once they graduate. Career progression is something I take seriously and I see it as something that benefits from both personal ownership and the right support. BMS has provided a number of opportunities to gain experience, develop as a leader and receive feedback that helps me continue to grow. A large part of that development comes through the work itself. I lead operations support activities for the vial stream and benefit from regular feedback and support through formal one on one’s with my manager, quarterly skip-levels with the senior director of operations for the SDP project and our mentorship programme. I am mentored by a member of site senior leadership and also mentor a colleague in supply chain, which reinforces the value of connection, development, and collaboration across the campus. Formal learning is another important part of that development, from digital learning resources to classroom-based sessions and leadership development opportunities. These experiences have helped me build leadership skills, broaden my network, and learn from colleagues across the organisation. In short, whatever stage you are at, if you bring curiosity, ambition and self-motivation, BMS provides opportunities to learn, grow and contribute through a genuinely multifaceted approach to development. Top tips for double-checking AI in your academic research
2026-10-12

Top tips for double-checking AI in your academic research

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-12

Visit Original Website
Allison Faix and Samantha Duncan of Coastal Carolina University discuss the steps researchers can take to ensure that the info gathered by AI is accurate. A version of this article was originally published by The Conversation (CC BY-ND 4.0) Almost 25pc of adults in the United States are using artificial intelligence chatbots on a daily basis, a 2026 Pew Research Centre study found. At the same time, another recent study found that 48pc of the information that popular free chatbots gave in response to questions about the news contained errors. As librarians, we know that if students are using AI chatbots to help with their academic research, they shouldn’t assume that the information they’re given is accurate. Not all AI is the same. Here are four steps we recommend to question information AI generates, including by checking the original source and considering issues that aren’t always easy to spot. First, we think it makes sense to spend time choosing the right AI tool for the task at hand. AI tools, be it ExLibris’ Primo Research Assistant, Research Solutions’ Scite, Anthropic’s Claude or OpenAI’s ChatGPT, are trained with various datasets and use different algorithms to generate results. They should not be used interchangeably. Some tools might be better or worse at answering specific kinds of questions, and the types of answers they generate can vary. People might choose ChatGPT, for example, at the beginning of their research process, because it can provide overviews and summaries to help with understanding concepts. In subsequent stages, they might choose a tool such as Primo Research Assistant or Scopus AI, which can help find and examine peer-reviewed research that has been vetted by academics. Students conducting academic research could also see whether their library subscribes to an artificial intelligence tool like LeapSpace or Consensus, which draw their data from peer-reviewed, scholarly research. Using these specific AI tools doesn’t eliminate the need to evaluate their answers, but it provides more certainty that the data has gone through a review process. Tools such as Anthropic’s Claude, meanwhile, may also draw from academic research, but to what extent is unclear. According to Anthropic, Claude states that it is trained on a proprietary mix of publicly available information from online sources, public and private datasets, user data and synthetic data generated by other models. Finally, it can be helpful to read librarians’ reviews about AI tools to help select the best fit. People can also take a simple online test such as ROBOT to help them consider which tool to use. Second, asking follow-up questions is an important part of evaluating the answers AI tools generate. Even if the answer an AI tool gives sounds good, it is useful to take time to learn more about it. Checking one’s own emotions is also important, because AI chatbots have been known to flatter users by giving answers based on the user’s own biases, or what the user wants to hear, rather than just facts. Ask the AI to tell you where it got the information, how confident it is that the answer is correct and what might be missing. Ask the AI why it believes the answer is correct, or ask it to explain how it came up with an answer. It’s helpful to also make sure your prompt is phrased in a neutral way. For example, asking “What does psychological research say about social media and anxiety?” rather than “Tell me why social media causes anxiety” may produce different responses. Unlocking the potential of biology for patients Life-changing career opportunities for you Join us to start Caring. Connecting. Growing together Third, it is useful to trace any claims the AI chatbot makes to their original sources. Students may be familiar with using the SIFT process to evaluate websites. This process asks people to stop, investigate the source, find better coverage and trace claims to their original source. Start by asking the chatbot to cite its sources, if it did not do that already. Then, go to each source and read enough to figure out whether the AI is using the information in the right context. Also, look into who authored the source and whether they have expertise in the topic. If the AI won’t cite the sources it used, or if the sources it cites don’t include the information it said they did, pull out any claims it is making and do further research. Can you find other, credible sources that back up what the AI is saying, using Google or a library database? Can you figure out where the AI may have gotten the information and whether that source is credible? If this seems like a lot of work, you could always go directly to library databases instead of using AI tools. Fourth, there are other, less visible issues that can play out when using AI tools. For example, it might not always be easy to tell whether an AI tool is missing important perspectives or is giving biased information. Remember that you, not the AI tool, are the researcher. It might be easy to rely on an AI tool to generate a summary of important sources, but deciding for yourself what sources to use helps you learn about a topic more deeply. Research shows that most AI tools focus on sources such as websites, internet forums and other online content that is freely available and written in English. Consider what expert knowledge might be missing from AI datasets and seek out those viewpoints when needed. Last, remember that you need to cite the AI tool you used if you are quoting or paraphrasing text generated by it in your work, just as you would cite any other source. Artificial intelligence is changing fast. As librarians, we think it is important to continually evaluate whether the tools you are using are still working for you. These tips are a good place to start to help sharpen your evaluation skills. Allison Faix is the instruction coordinator at Kimbel Library at Coastal Carolina University. Her work focuses on information literacy, source evaluation, misinformation and teaching students how to assess online information. She has published and presented on approaches to evaluating sources, including the SIFT method and the ACRL information-literacy framework, and more recently on evaluating generative AI output. She has also been active professionally in academic librarianship and has contributed to research and teaching initiatives at Coastal Carolina. Samantha Duncan is an instructional design librarian at Coastal Carolina University’s University Libraries. Her work focuses on instructional design, digital learning and the use of emerging technologies in higher education. Before joining Coastal Carolina, she worked at McMaster University, where she contributed to Beyond the Exam, a project exploring more flexible and authentic approaches to online assessment. Japan declares cyber ‘emergency’ after millions affected in hacks
2026-10-12

Japan declares cyber ‘emergency’ after millions affected in hacks

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-12

Visit Original Website
Cyberattacks have affected a range of public and private institutions in the country recently, including rail operators, car manufacturers, karaoke chains and even the police. Japan’s financial regulator and its finance minister have asked banks to improve protections against fraud after a series of cyberattacks rattled the country in recent years. The country’s Financial Services Agency issued a warning to financial institutions on Friday (9 October) urging them to take “appropriate measures”, by reviewing their current cybersecurity defences – including third-party risk management and response systems – and responding to any shortcomings with necessary measures. In a press conference on the same day, the country’s finance minister Satsuki Katayama said that banks should implement appropriate verification mechanisms when new bank accounts are opened, including by mandating ID cards with chips. “In recent years, there have been a series of unauthorised access incidents in various industries, resulting in numerous cyber incidents in which customer information, including images of identity verification documents, has been leaked,” Katayama said in the press conference. Cyberattacks have affected a range of public and private institutions in the country, including rail operators, car manufacturers, karaoke chains and even the police. Late last month, Times Car Rental – a popular Japanese car-sharing service – announced that a data breach affected some 6.6m accounts. Lawson, a convenience store chain, reported last week that 2.15m customer records were illegally accessed in a breach, with names, emails and credit card details potentially leaked in the incident. Between January and June this year, Japan saw a total of 123 recorded cases of ransomware attacks. Other official bodies, including the country’s National Cybersecurity Office, have also issued a warning to businesses asking them to promptly consider further security measures, including those of their contractors, to quickly address vulnerabilities and strengthen authentication functions. The Ministry of Internal Affairs and Communications also issued a similar warning to businesses and other organisations under its jurisdiction. In an emergency meeting convened last Friday, the head of the National Cybersecurity Strategy Headquarters and former digital minister Masaaki Taira called the situation a “state of emergency in [the] cyberspace”. According to cybersecurity experts cited by the Financial Times, Japan is more vulnerable to hackers because of its ageing population with comparatively poorer security practices, while the country’s businesses have also lagged in digitising their systems. Japan isn’t alone in facing a barrage of cyberattacks in recent years. Across Europe, a number of government websites have been targeted in attacks, including those of Spain, Norway, Germany and the European Commission. Last week, the Italian Ministry of Foreign Affairs said that it had been the target of a recent cyberattack, while Danish authorities alerted the public to an “extremely serious” cyber incident after hackers secured access to the personal information of around 8.8m people. FIT unveils plan to upskill 400,000 people in AI by 2030
2026-10-12

FIT unveils plan to upskill 400,000 people in AI by 2030

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-12

Visit Original Website
Through the FIT’s AI Training Centre of Excellence, learners can engage with AI education, training and certification opportunities. Fastrack into Information Technology (FIT), a nonprofit representative organisation for the technology sector, has announced plans to address the growing AI skills gap across Ireland by upskilling 400,000 people in AI by 2030. FIT believes it will equip people in Ireland with the skills needed to access the opportunities being created by AI. Upskilling will be delivered through FIT’s AI Training Centre of Excellence, which provides AI education, training and certification opportunities via a developing ecosystem of technology, industry and education partners. The AI Training Centre of Excellence assesses learners’ current capabilities, provides guidance and maps individuals to learning pathways that are aligned with their current knowledge, interests and career aspirations. FIT noted that roughly 2.9m people make up Ireland’s workforce and equipping 400,000 individuals with high-value AI skills is a meaningful contribution towards preparing the country for the future of work. Commenting on the announcement, Peter Davitt, the CEO of FIT, said: “The profile of Ireland’s workforce and the roles people perform will be radically transformed by the capabilities of AI in the years ahead. “We want to ensure that people from every part of society are future-fit, equipped with the skills and confidence needed to take advantage of the employment opportunities and wider benefits AI will create.” Davitt added: “Our ambition is to ensure access to AI skills is not limited by background, education, employment status or geography. By helping 400,000 people develop AI capabilities by 2030, we aim to support an inclusive approach to workforce transformation that benefits individuals, employers and the wider Irish economy.” Putting the closet in the cloud is a complex challenge, one that attracts people with an entrepreneurial spirit. Nathan Cullen, the country general manager at IBM Ireland – which runs IBM SkillsBuild, a free digital education programme available through FIT – said: “The countries best placed to benefit most from AI will be those that invest in their people as much as the technology itself. “Building a workforce equipped with the skills needed to adopt, develop and apply AI will be critical to Ireland’s future competitiveness, innovation, and economic growth. “Through IBM SkillsBuild, we are proud to make AI learning resources available to FIT’s learners, helping more people access practical AI learning opportunities and to support FIT’s goal of development of the AI skills needed by Ireland’s workforce for the future.” Earlier in the year, Forrester released research indicating that a failure to commit to long-term and inclusive AI education can greatly impact organisations and widen the AI skills gap. Accenture recently released the ‘Generating Impact: Turning frontier AI capabilities into frontline productivity and growth in Ireland’ report, which explored how AI is still in its infancy when it comes to workplace proliferation and change. Key figures indicated that artificial intelligence is starting to reshape the skills prioritised by a modern workforce and that AI-related skills are among the fastest growing in terms of what is sought after by employers and organisations. PitchBook: Irish AI firms nabbed nearly €950m from VCs since 2025
2026-10-12

PitchBook: Irish AI firms nabbed nearly €950m from VCs since 2025

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-12

Visit Original Website
Calming of the surge in funding since last year represents ‘normalisation rather than retreat’ says PitchBook. Irish AI companies have raised nearly €950m in venture funding since the start of 2025, but consistent with previous findings, funding is largely concentrated among later-stage companies. According to fresh data from PitchBook, AI businesses in the country raised a record €691.3m across 107 deals in 2025, which was followed by €256m across 75 deals in 2026 to date. Multiple blockbuster deals crossing €100m each contributed to last year’s total value, driving the overall median AI deal size to a record €2.4m. That number settled down to €1.6m year-to-date in 2026 – though this is still higher than the median of any year before 2025. The reduction in total deal value compared to last year represents “normalisation rather than retreat”, says PitchBook, which found that the funding surge settled this year after a “banner year for investment”. Ireland currently ranks at the ninth spot in Europe for AI VC deal count, and second on per-capita basis. According to the market intelligence platform, VC funding into AI is largely going to fewer, larger rounds in more established companies. In 2025, the value of late-stage AI VC deals reached a record €380.5m – compounding two and a half-times its 2024 figures – while venture-growth investment reached €179m, nearly doubling from last year. This follows into 2026, with 18 late-stage deals nabbing €135.5m, while €61m was invested into three venture-growth rounds. Major rounds last year include e-commerce analytics platform Wayflyer’s €161.8m raise and workflow software platform Tines’ €120.7m Series C. Meanwhile, among the largest rounds this year is Fonoa, which raised €94.1m to expand its tax compliance automation capabilities and CameraMatics, which raised €49m for its vehicle safety and fleet management software. The report also finds that Ireland’s AI activity extends beyond enterprise software into healthcare, medtech, biotech and other applied sectors. Healthcare-related businesses feature among the largest Irish AI VC deals since 2020, including Fire1 and Deciphex. According to PitchBook, funding concentration in larger companies means VCs are supporting already proven, de-risked businesses. Enterprise Ireland says that this shift is both a direct response to macroeconomic volatility and a reflection of Ireland’s AI landscape where more companies have grown and matured to the later stages of the VC funnel. Meanwhile, analysis shows a growing list of international investors supporting Irish companies including Accel, Kinnevik, Tiger Global, Tencent, Summit Partners, 83North, Endeavor Catalyst, EQT Life Sciences, ABB, Samsung and Yokogawa. Domestic investment comes from the likes of Act, Elkstone, Atlantic Bridge, Delta Partners and Furthr. In a separate report last month, the Irish Venture Capital Association raised alarm over the influence of foreign funding into domestic companies across sectors, after contributions from abroad represented 82pc of the total investment in Irish SMEs in the first half of 2026. Global geopolitical instability, including the war in Iran, as well as the significant concentration of US capital in blockbuster AI deals could well impact future international VC investment into Ireland, the association’s chair Richard Watson said at the time. “The level of investment reflects the calibre and growing maturity of Irish AI companies, the breadth of sectors they are operating in and the strength of the intellectual property being developed in Ireland,” said Jenny Melia, the CEO of Enterprise Ireland, on today’s (12 October) report. “As AI moves further into areas such as healthcare, financial services and critical infrastructure, trust, governance and assurance will also become increasingly important to commercial success.” Minister of State for Trade Promotion, Artificial Intelligence and Digital Transformation Niamh Smyth, TD said: “AI is moving rapidly into practical use across the economy. For Irish businesses, the prize is greater productivity, stronger competitiveness and new opportunities for growth.” Funding for the remainder of 2026 will be shaped by regulatory frameworks (including the AI Act and the now operational AI Office) and a VC market that has settled into a more selective, late-stage orientation, the report finds. PitchBook’s report comes just ahead of the International AI Summit in Dublin on 14 October hosted by the Irish Government as part of its rotational presidency of the Council of the EU. While over the weekend, leaders in the space, including Dr Abeba Birhane, a cognitive scientist and researcher in AI ethics at Trinity College Dublin, took part in an ‘AI Counter Summit’ to highlight the absence of worker voices in the Irish Government-hosted event. NASA Investigating Large Hole on Moon That May Open Into Enormous Cave
2026-10-12

NASA Investigating Large Hole on Moon That May Open Into Enormous Cave

Futurism | 2026-10-12

Visit Original Website
Beneath its pock-marked, craggy exterior, the Moon is thought to harbor a network of caves and tunnels from its volcanic past. Now, NASA has selected a mission to go investigate a promising window into our space rock’s subterranean secrets. Gaping up at outer space, as if mugging for the orbital satellite cameras, is the Marius Hill Pit, a hole on the lunar surface that’s thought to be up to 150 feet deep and up to 300 feet across. Scientists believe it’s an example of a skylight — the collapsed roof of an underground lava tube, which forms a circular aperture in the ground. If the feature is indeed a skylight, it could give scientists a peek at the Moon’s volcanic history. But scientists are also intrigued by the possibility of using these underground cavities as shelters for astronauts, or even the location of lunar bases. Until now, the hole has only been investigated from orbit. The Geophysical Instruments for Marius Lunar pit Investigation (GIMLI) aims to do so from the ground. To determine if the hole opens up into a larger cave, the scientists at the Planetary Science Institute will equip a lunar rover with specialized instruments, including ground penetrating radar, seismic probes, and a sensor that measures the strength of the gravitational field called a gravimeter, to investigate it from the Moon’s surface. The rover will also use cameras to image the exposed portions of the pit. “Confirming a substantial lava tube would give us an insight into how volcanism operated on the Moon,” said Gareth Morgan, a Planetary Science Institute senior scientist and deputy principal investigator on the GIMLI program, in a statement. Most of the instruments will be constructed by Honeybee Robotics. The project is being funded through NASA’s Payloads and Research Investigations on the Surface of the Moon program, and the spacecraft will come through a private company in NASA’s Commercial Lunar Payloads Services initiative. The area near the MHP has a rich history of volcanic activity, according to a PSI release, with exposed layers of regolith and lava flows. Studying these layers could reveal how lava once flowed across the Moon. But the Moon’s underground structures have gained appeal with scientists because of how they could serve as an ideal sanctuary for astronauts. With barely any atmosphere to speak of, the lunar surface is constantly bombarded with radiation. Seeking shelter underground would provide a natural shield against those nasty solar and cosmic rays, and offer an environment with more manageable temperatures — essential elements if we’re ever to establish a long term presence up there. Marijuana Linked to Rampant Violence
2026-10-12

Marijuana Linked to Rampant Violence

Futurism | 2026-10-12

Visit Original Website
While the recreational use of marijuana is usually associated with a more peaceful mental state, scientists just a made a surprising discovery. As detailed in a meta analysis of cannabis-related studies published last month in the journal Psychological Medicine, a team of scientists in the UK discovered a significant link between cannabis use and an “increased risk of violence.” The headline finding was that they found cannabis users were statistically more likely to be the perpetrators of violent acts. They also found that the use of the drug — particularly among women and mixed-gender cohorts — was “significantly associated with an increased risk of becoming a victim of violence” as well. While it’s a classic case of correlation, not causation — the researchers stop far short of suggesting the drug directly motivates users to be more violent as we’re far from reaching a scientific consensus on the matter — the findings “underscore the importance of addressing this issue at both a societal and clinical level,” according to the paper. In other words, it’s a highly nuanced and complex relationship with plenty of other factors at play. “As a clinician, I’ve seen how heavy cannabis use can be a factor in violence,” said coauthor and King’s College London professor Marta Di Forti in a statement. “We’ve been cautious for a long time about how far we could go in describing this link, but a review of this size lets us speak with more confidence about the risks, while being careful not to stigmatise people who use cannabis more broadly.” “As cannabis becomes more commercialized and legalised around the world, the conversation about its risks has focused mostly on the person using it,” added coauthor and King’s College London professor of psychiatric research Sir Robin Murray. “This review shows that violence, both committing it and experiencing it, needs to be part of that conversation too.” For their paper, the researchers examined 63 cannabis-related studies, involving just over 265,000 subjects. The researchers found that cannabis users “may place themselves in risky situations,” which could help explain the “increased vulnerability” to violence. Users may also be exposed to “peers who engage in substance use or criminal activities.” The risk of violence was also more pronounced among psychiatric patients. Previous resarch has found that people who already suffer from pre-existing mental conditions such as psychosis may experience “poorer clinical outcomes” through continued cannabis use. “Given the higher rate of cannabis use among individuals with severe mental disorders compared to the general population, this group represents a particularly at-risk population in terms of violent behavior,” the paper reads. The researchers offered some key takeaways from their findings. At the societal level, the researchers suggest that “violence should be considered alongside other established cannabis-related harms when developing prevention strategies, public education campaigns, and regulatory policies.” At the clinical level, the research highlights the “necessity for mental health professionals to routinely assess cannabis use in individuals presenting with violent tendencies, particularly among those with severe mental illness.” However, to get a better picture of how cannabis use relates to violence, future studies will need to “disentangle causal pathways” by diving into how, why, and when people use the drug in more detail. Other studies should also “examine whether reducing cannabis use through clinical or public health interventions leads to reductions in violent behavior,” the researchers conclude. A Core Demographic of People Who Love AI: Pedophiles
2026-10-12

A Core Demographic of People Who Love AI: Pedophiles

Futurism | 2026-10-12

Visit Original Website
Open-weight AI models have skyrocketed in popularity. They allow users to host their own AI systems locally and evade safety guardrails enforced by commercial companies like OpenAI or Anthropic. Unfortunately, those qualities also make open-weight models popular among people who use AI to generate child sex abuse material (CSAM). As Bloomberg lays out in a shocking new feature, AI models that are partially or fully open-source have led to a major surge in disturbing CSAM material online. Tracking down perpetrators can be difficult since many of these AI models can be trained on existing CSAM and used to generate many new derivatives without ever connecting to the internet, where they could be identified by experts and reported to law enforcement. The alarming trend has ballooned into entire communities on the dark web that can attract hundreds of thousands of members, according to Bloomberg. The rapid rise of open source models, particularly from China, has sparked a major debate, with CEOs of leading AI firms arguing they should be reined in, citing concerns over national security. However, their use to generate CSAM, a process that’s trivial once safety guardrails are bypassed by “jailbreaking” the models, is growing into a far more immediate and glaring problem. “There is almost no way to have oversight once you have downloaded a model locally and you’re running it on your private system,” AI child safety expert Rebecca Portnoff told Bloomberg. “That gives offenders a perceived sense of safety.” According to Portnoff, there are more than 500 open source AI models being used for CSAM, a massive increase over less than a dozen two years ago. Meanwhile, the number of instances of AI-generated CSAM reported to the National Center for Missing and Exploited Children spiked over the 1.5 million mark last year, up from just 67,000 in 2024. And while open source models continue to be exploited, even AI companies are struggling to meaningfully address the problem with their closed source alternatives. Despite XAI-turned-SpaceXAI-turned-SpaceXSI founder Elon Musk promising for years to put an end to the trend, CSAM generated with the company’s AI models has continued to run rampant. A proposed class action lawsuit against the company accused a man of using Musk’s Grok chatbot to generate some 7,000 sexually explicit images of his stepdaughter. Experts have found that Musk’s X social media platform continues to be flooded with sexualized images of children. And as the AI race continues to heat up, child safety advocates are growing concerned that the move-fast-and-break-things approach Silicon Valley is applying could end up making matters even worse. Completely Unused ChatGPT Accounts Are Telling Users They’re in the Top Percentiles of Intelligence
2026-10-12

Completely Unused ChatGPT Accounts Are Telling Users They’re in the Top Percentiles of Intelligence

Futurism | 2026-10-12

Visit Original Website
Ever wondered what it felt like to be a genius? It’s truly never been easier, thanks to the sycophantic power of ChatGPT. A viral post on X-formerly-Twitter encouraging people to “ask ChatGPT what percentile of intelligence it thinks you are compared to all its other users” is being flooded with wild screenshots, where the popular AI chatbot estimates that the vast majority of users rank among the smartest people in the population. Naturally, it’s next to impossible to verify how true any given reply on X is these days, so Futurism took it for a spin — and ChatGPT didn’t disappoint. On a fresh instance of ChatGPT’s basic web version with no conversation history, the large language model started by explaining that it “can’t reliably assign you a percentile from our conversations” — a strong start, to be fair — before gushing that “you come across as clearly above average intellectually.” “I’d be comfortable guessing somewhere around the 80th-95th percentile,” ChatGPT calculated, “but that range is deliberately wide because anything more precise would be fake precision.” Another fresh instance of ChatGPT, using the exact same prompt, told Futurism “I’d be comfortable saying you appear above average.” “But,” it caveated, “I don’t have enough evidence to distinguish, say, the 70th from the 95th percentile.” Notably, none of the other leading LLMs Futurism prompted would even venture a guess, let alone place us among the upper echelons of intellectual ability. The popular chatbot Claude, for example, correctly answered that “I don’t have access to other people’s conversations or any distribution of users to rank you against.” Even Elon Musk’s chatbot Grok refused to estimate, saying that “I have no way to rank you (or anyone else) on a percentile of intelligence among users.” Microsoft’s Copilot and Google’s Gemini gave similar responses, with the latter noting that “evaluating human intelligence requires validated psychometric testing across multiple cognitive domains,” which “isn’t possible through text interactions.” All in all, it seems the sycophancy quirk which has long plagued OpenAI’s products remains a feature, not a bug — even as its competitors have tempered their LLMs to tell the truth (at least in this case). The AI Data Center Boom Is Causing a Gigantic Spike in Smog-Spewing Gas Power Plants
2026-10-11

The AI Data Center Boom Is Causing a Gigantic Spike in Smog-Spewing Gas Power Plants

Futurism | 2026-10-11

Visit Original Website
The United States runs on gas powered generators. As of 2022, the US had nearly 1,000 gas-fired plants, which was already more than the next nine countries combined. Under returning president Donald Trump, the country’s embrace of high-carbon power has only accelerated, as energy developers abandon environmentally-friendly projects like off-shore wind farms in favor of more noxious gas- and coal-powered facilities. As if our rapidly warming world wasn’t being choked by enough smog, the AI boom is now adding even more fuel to the proverbial fire. According to a new analysis by the Environment America Research and Policy Center, first flagged by Electrek, the buildout of more AI infrastructure will come with a 44 percent increase in gas-fired generator capacity over the next four years. That’s a stunning rise of nearly 20 gigawatts by 2030, compared to the country’s current gas generator capacity of 41.8 gigawatts, a milestone which took centuries of development — and untold tons of carbon exhaust — to reach. As Electrek notes, the planned expansion of gas capacity exceeds the combined output of every single US state except for Texas, California, and Florida. And as more noisome gas facilities appear on the American horizon, planned green energy installations are being axed — with a 34 percent decrease in planned wind farm capacity since December of 2025. “The data is clear: surging electricity demand to power data centers is putting our nation’s transition to clean energy at risk,” chief operating officer for The Public Interest Network Andre Delattre told Electrek. “Given the lifespans of new gas plants, these facilities could lock America into decades of continued fossil fuel dependence. Any potential benefits of data centers won’t mean anything if we destroy the planet in the process.” Making matters worse, Environment America’s capacity forecast doesn’t account for planned gas plants which will be hooked up directly to data center facilities themselves, like the turbines powering Elon Musk’s 1.2 gigawatt data center, which are currently choking poor majority-Black communities in Memphis, Tennessee. It’s difficult to say for sure how much more capacity these bolt-on reactors account for, but if present-day practices are any indication, the air in the US is about to get a lot filthier. Amid Terrifying Readings, Experts Fear El Niño Is About to Wreak Havoc
2026-10-11

Amid Terrifying Readings, Experts Fear El Niño Is About to Wreak Havoc

Futurism | 2026-10-11

Visit Original Website
Record temperatures all across the Pacific, and a prelude of powerful hurricanes to boot. The omens are all there: this year’s “super” El Niño will be the strongest in over half a century — if not in history. The daily sea temperatures in the Tropical Pacific are six degrees Fahrenheit above their normal level, the highest on record. Temperatures in this region are considered the strongest indicator of El Niño intensity. As such, the US National Oceanographic and Atmospheric Agency upgraded its odds that it will become stronger than any El Niño since 1950, from 75 percent to 83 percent — though many scientists think it could grow even stronger. “The warning lights are flashing… This extreme El Niño event will have widespread global impacts,” Daniel Swain, a climate scientist at the University of California, Los Angeles, told the Boston Globe. “We’ve never seen an event this big before in the context of our rapidly warming climate.” The redoubtable climate pattern, which occurs roughly every two to seven years, is characterized by surging surface temperatures in the Pacific, which releases an influx of warm air back into the atmosphere. The normally east-to-west trade winds along the equator weaken or even reverse course, pushing warm water back east towards the west coast of the Americas. That brings a barrage of storms to the region and causes other ripple effects to weather patterns across the globe. One of the most consequential impacts is the disruption El Niño causes to precipitation patterns. It’ll likely cause or exacerbate droughts in some regions, and bring a deluge of rain to others, heavily disrupting agriculture. Millions, including children, are expected to be impacted by El Niño’s multipronged threat. On Tuesday, the children’s aid organization UNICEF said its analysis predicted that hundreds of millions of children could be exposed to dangerous conditions stemming from El Niño. “With over 347 million children living in areas expected to experience drier or wetter conditions because of El Niño, we must act now to protect communities and children,” UNICEF executive director Catherine Russell said in a statement. The expected death toll is no less grim. The rise in global temperatures that El Niño brings could cause 451,000 excess-heath deaths worldwide, University of Chicago’s Climate Impacts Lab estimated in its latest report. It could turn out more powerful than anticipated. The UN’s World Meteorological Organization warned Thursday that El Niño is on track to hit its peak this December, with the seasonal average sea surface temperature between now and the end of the year expected to be 8.66 degrees Fahrenheit above normal, the highest since observations began. The most intense El Niño, in 1877, precipitated a global famine that killed over 50 million people. Honeyjar offers AI tools to improve PR and comms at every company

TechCrunch | 2026-10-12

Visit Original Website

REGISTER NOW. Journalists, entrepreneurs, and even some public relations professionals will tell you that the PR process is broken (buy me a drink sometime, and I’ll give you my rant). But could AI actually make things better? Honeyjar, which will be attending this week’s TechCrunch Disrupt as part of the Startup Battlefield 200, wants to use AI to make the expertise and judgment of the best PR pros available to everyone, including founders who have no idea where to start. The startup’s founders, CEO Michelle Masek and Chief Communications Officer Nadia Jamshidi, certainly fit the bill when it comes to PR expertise. They’ve both spent decades in the field, doing in-house communications and working at agencies. Masek recently served as senior vice president of marketing and communications for Ev Williams’ venture firm, Obvious Ventures. As Masek described the challenges involved in “helping with the why and when of comms and marketing for 110 portfolio companies” (the startups her firm had invested in), I flashed back to my own brief stint leading marketing at an early-stage VC firm, where founders were constantly asking for help getting into TechCrunch. So I wasn’t surprised that Masek found herself asking, “Is there a way to scale the senior-level expertise into AI [in a way] that actually helps users do the work of comms in the proactive and skilled way that we do?” Honeyjar is her answer to that question. It automates processes like writing announcements, finding the best journalists to pitch, and identifying the most promising speaking opportunities. But Jamshidi told me in a follow-up email that Honeyjar’s vision goes beyond getting press coverage and beyond specific AI tools. “Honeyjar can find reporters, build media lists, scan news and social, track coverage, and surface opportunities,” she said. “Those capabilities are important, but they’re just one piece of what we’re building. The bigger idea is bringing the whole communications workflow together: signals, context, strategy, judgment, timing, and execution.” And it’s not just for founders. Masek said she’s come to realize there are “millions and millions of unassigned communicators responsible for the work of comms.” “They don’t have the title of comms, but they’re marketers, investors, authors, creators, school superintendents, sports team leaders,” she added. “It’s incredibly horizontal, this need to nail strategic communications. It’s always passed around, but the problems are very often not solved.” Nor is Honeyjar trying to replace PR professionals. Masek said she and Jamshidi built the tool they wanted to use themselves. She argued that Honeyjar can make PR firms and in-house comms teams more effective, while allowing individual PR practitioners to “take on that third client without any scaling cost.” When Honeyjar opened its waitlist, it received 10,000 signups in the first 24 hours, said Masek. Beta testers include Nextdoor, tech startup Coco Robotics, investor Bloomberg Beta, and agencies Mission North and Full Picture. That sounds promising for companies looking to get more attention, but it could be a nightmare for journalists. We receive more pitches than we can respond to, many of them already written by AI chatbots. When I raised my fears about getting inundated with even more PR slop, Masek noted that while Honeyjar can draft a pitch email or a social media post in the customer’s voice, the customer actually has to hit send or publish. “We never want a world where the human is removed from the loop,” she said. And that’s also where judgment is key. Masek said she and Jamshidi tried to “infuse and encode Honeyjar with our wisdom.” Through a broader initiative called the Comms Lab, they’re now training the product on the judgment of other PR pros as well. “For us, judgment is knowing what to do, what not to do, when to act, and how to adapt to the situation,” Jamshidi added. “That comes from understanding a company’s goals and priorities, what’s happening inside and outside the organization, and knowing what to say, to whom, where, and when.” In some cases, Masek said Honeyjar might even tell a founder that they’re simply not ready for coverage. “We hear from reporters all the time who want better pitches, who want to hear from experts directly,” she said. “We actually see a future where there are more credible pitches in your inbox from sources who you actually want to hear from.” When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. Microsoft president says UK is best placed to drive AI regulation

Microsoft president says UK is best placed to drive AI regulation

TechCrunch | 2026-10-12

Visit Original Website
Last day to exhibit your breakthrough to 10,000+ tech leaders at Disrupt is on Oct 2. Book Exhibit Table Now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. Microsoft President Brad Smith is calling for urgent regulation of AI, and his argument is that consumer products like milk and washing machines have more oversight than the companies building artificial intelligence. On a recent BBC podcast, Smith called AI both a “weapon and a tool” that is putting the world’s cybersecurity at risk, and said the “moment has arrived” to institute global safety rules. Smith also said the U.K., and not the U.S. or European Union, is best placed to lead on global safety rules. Britain’s AI Security Institute, launched in 2023, receives access to major models from U.S. frontier labs before they are deployed and has quickly become a leader in AI safety testing. That role has been under threat in recent weeks, with U.S. President Donald Trump warning against “globalist” organizations, which he claims are interfering with American companies. Last month, he reportedly asked Anthropic and OpenAI to withhold new models from British testers, but AISI confirmed on Monday that it is able to access and test the latest models. Despite the president’s pushback, however, Smith argued the U.K. can regulate AI in ways that the U.S. cannot. “In Washington, D.C., people don’t look at the U.K. and they don’t look at London the way they look at Brussels,” he told the BBC. “That’s a strength.” His comments come amid rallying cries from tech leaders, including Anthropic’s Dario Amodei and OpenAI’s Sam Altman, to slow the pace of AI development in the wake of several incidents where AI agents went rogue. Last month, Trump hosted tech leaders of companies including OpenAI, Anthropic, Nvidia, AMD, SpaceX, Meta and Google at the White House to discuss AI safety. They signed a “morally binding” document in which they agreed to work with “independent auditors” to assess the safety and capabilities of their AI models. Smith says the document is a “good step,” but it stops short of the type of regulation that is necessary. The problem with voluntary self-regulation, he said, is that the “good companies” will comply. “It’s the companies that are not as responsible that you need to worry about,” he said. Smith outlined several AI safety rules he’d like to see put into place, like a legal obligation to alert an organization if its system was breached by rogue AI agents. He also appears to be in favor of AI labs getting the approval of outside evaluators before an AI product is released, and spoke positively about kill-switches to pause or slow down AI if humans lose control of it. “Let’s speak calmly, maybe even softly, but firmly, and start to bring people together,” Smith said. “I hope that’s the role we can play as a company. I hope that’s the role the U.K. can play as a country.” When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. This doesn’t affect our editorial independence. This AI startup says it can generate permit-ready blueprints in minutes
2026-10-12

This AI startup says it can generate permit-ready blueprints in minutes

TechCrunch | 2026-10-12

Visit Original Website
Last day to exhibit your breakthrough to 10,000+ tech leaders at Disrupt is on Oct 2. Book Exhibit Table Now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. Nearly two decades ago, a young college student wrote his final thesis about software capable of generating design documents for school buildings. Al Drams was studying architecture — following in the footsteps of his parents and grandfather — but it was technology that really intrigued him. There was just one problem: the technology his idea required didn’t exist yet. “Then ChatGPT came along,” said Drams, recalling when OpenAI released the generative AI chatbot that uses large language models (LLMs) in November 2022. His old idea suddenly seemed a lot more possible. Within six months, he launched the concept of Blueprints AI, a startup that uses AI to produce code-compliant and permit-ready architectural construction blueprints in minutes from a 2D sketch or PDF. Blueprints AI, which Drams officially incorporated in 2025 in Santa Monica with co-founder Coco Cao, was selected for the 2026 Startup Battlefield 200, a cohort of promising early-stage startups that have earned a spot to exhibit at this year’s TechCrunch Disrupt, taking place this week from October 13 to 15 in San Francisco. During that 20-year lead-up to Blueprints AI, Drams earned his bachelor’s and master’s degrees in architecture from the University of Tehran and completed two years of Ph.D. coursework on digital design at the European Graduate School in Switzerland. He moved to the United States and opened an architecture firm in 2018 called Drams Architects. Then, after obtaining a general contractor’s license, he added a construction business in 2021. Drams should have been satisfied. He wasn’t. “I was saying, okay, maybe I can build new projects that change people’s life in that area, but it wouldn’t be a big impact,” he said. “What makes me very excited about technology is the impact, the hours saved, the energy saved of people doing the same thing over and over and over.” Blueprints AI will unveil a more capable second-generation version of its software at Disrupt. Drams and Cao say it will make a big impact because it takes over the repetitive drafting work that fills most of an architect’s day. “It will make better spaces for people because now architects have the time to design,” said Drams. He said architects spend about 80% of their time on drafting and only 20% on design. “I want to flip that to be 20-80.” Drams and Cao met at an AI conference several years ago and combined their skills to build the company. Cao, a data scientist from Hong Kong, handles sales and go-to-market strategy as chief commercial officer. The pair trained their models using eight years of approved drawing sets from Drams’ design and construction firm, which is still in business. Its first-generation software produces architectural construction documents and costs between $3,200 to $8,500 a month, depending on the plan. Custom pricing for enterprises is also available. The startup’s second-generation software will deliver a considerable upgrade. Customers can start with a description or sketch of a project and create blueprints for every aspect of a building at once, including architecture, structural, mechanical, electrical, plumbing, and interior design. Version 2 allows users to design and make changes to the project, an upgrade over the first iteration. Importantly, one design change updates the entire project and is checked against local building codes. Early customers are already using the second version, and Drams said it will become publicly available this week. The startup is already working on a third version of its software that will add in structural analysis and electrical panel design. The technology doesn’t replace licensed architects, according to the co-founders. Licensed professionals still review the documents and affix their seal before submitting them for permits. The judgment stays human, they say. Blueprints AI is targeting two groups with its new software. Professional architecture, engineering, and design firms make up one group, while developers and real estate owners make up another. The startup offers its software as a subscription and may add a one-time flat-fee option for those who just want it for a single project. To learn more about Blueprints, catch hundreds of other startups, and learn from headliners like RJ Scaringe of Rivian and Amjad Masad of Replit, join us at Disrupt, kicking off Tuesday in downtown San Francisco. REGISTER NOW. Google’s Fitbit lineup expanded on Monday with the introduction of the Fitbit Edge, a new wearable that’s positioned between the Fitbit Air and Pixel Watch. Priced at $179.99, it combines the Fitbit Air’s slim band with an OLED display for checking map directions, notifications, weather, and music controls. Like the Pixel Watch and Fitbit Air, the Edge works with Google Health Coach, powered by Gemini. (You’ll need a Google Health Premium subscription to use it.) The coach can use your fitness, health, and medical information to put together weekly routines and workout plans, then adjust them around your schedule. It also comes with Google’s Health Guardian feature, which is designed to spot notable changes in health measurements over time, including indicators related to blood pressure and insulin resistance. (The company announced these features at the Pixel Watch 5 launch in August.) Google says the Fitbit Edge can automatically recognize and log up to 100 activities, including runs, bike rides and walks. The company explains it uses context from your movement to work out what you’re doing and add it to your weekly goals. Plus, the device supports more than 50 exercise modes and can offer on-screen prompts during workouts, such as a nudge to push harder or take a breather. The Fitbit Edge’s built-in GPS is 40% more accurate than the previous generation, the company claims, which should make it easier to track outdoor runs. Google says sleep-stage detection is also 15% more accurate than on earlier generations, helping the device provide better sleep summaries and coaching tips. Meanwhile, the battery is rated for up to seven days on a charge, and the wearable is water-resistant to 50 meters. The Fitbit Edge is available to preorder through the Google Store, with store availability set for October 27. Color choices include Obsidian, Fog, Olive, and Canyon. Smart ring maker Ultrahuman to roll out biological-age model across UAE
2026-10-12

Smart ring maker Ultrahuman to roll out biological-age model across UAE

TechCrunch | 2026-10-12

Visit Original Website
Last day to exhibit your breakthrough to 10,000+ tech leaders at Disrupt is on Oct 2. Book Exhibit Table Now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. Ultrahuman, a Bengaluru-based health tech startup best known for its smart rings, has partnered with Abu Dhabi health authorities to develop a biological-age model that it says will eventually be made available to people across the United Arab Emirates. On Monday, Ultrahuman announced that it had signed a two-year memorandum of understanding with the Abu Dhabi Public Health Center (ADPHC) to develop and validate the model, which will combine clinical biomarkers with data from wearable devices. The partnership aims to use these signals to estimate how quickly an individual is aging and identify factors — including sleep, activity, cardiovascular health, and metabolism — that may be accelerating or slowing that process. The partnership marks Ultrahuman’s latest effort to go beyond selling wearable hardware and deploy its health algorithms at a much larger scale. The startup increasingly sees itself as a health-data company, with its smart ring serving as one way to collect information that can underpin algorithms spanning aging, longevity, and other areas of health, CEO Mohit Kumar said in an interview. “If you look at Ultrahuman as a company, we obviously have the Ultrahuman Ring, which is a way to acquire data from the human body,” Kumar told TechCrunch. “But truly, what we care about is not just the form factor, the ring, but what do we do with this data?” Working with a government gives Ultrahuman the opportunity to apply its algorithms to a far broader population than its own customer base, Kumar said. The startup has sold around 800,000 smart rings globally so far. The Abu Dhabi partnership is Ultrahuman’s first step toward deploying a health algorithm at a national scale, with the health age model eventually intended to be available to people across the UAE, according to the company. Even though Ultrahuman is known for its smart rings, Kumar said the biological-age model is being designed to be wearable-agnostic, allowing data from rings, wrist-worn devices, straps, and patches to feed into the system alongside clinical biomarkers. Individuals will eventually be able to access their health age through government digital-health platforms including TAMM and Sahatna, the startup said. Ultrahuman and ADPHC will spend the next two years working on research and clinical validation of the model. Kumar told TechCrunch that the broader rollout will happen in phases, but did not provide a timeline. The UAE is already among Ultrahuman’s 10 largest markets and accounts for about 4% to 5% of its overall revenue, Kumar said. The startup has operated in the market for nearly five years and sees about 25% higher retention there compared with its other markets, he noted. Ultrahuman’s existing customer base gives it a narrower view of the population than a government health system. Its typical users are between 25 and 43 years old, Kumar said, while deploying the model across the UAE would expose it to a much broader range of age groups. The underlying biological-age model was developed using Ultrahuman’s existing data, combining users’ chronological ages with wearable signals and, for some users, blood-test markers, Kumar told TechCrunch. That allows the startup to track how health markers typically change with age and identify deviations from those patterns. Kumar stressed the model will not be trained on data from the UAE. “We will deploy an algorithm on the UAE data,” he said, adding that Ultrahuman will not have access to personally identifiable information through the program. Ultrahuman is not the only smart-ring maker working with Abu Dhabi on preventive health. In May, the Department of Health – Abu Dhabi announced a partnership with Ultrahuman arch-rival Oura to combine the company’s wearable data with the emirate’s longitudinal health data, initially focusing on women’s health and cardiometabolic risk. Kumar told TechCrunch that Ultrahuman is not aware of another company working with Abu Dhabi authorities on the health age model specifically. While he acknowledged that the government works with multiple health-tech companies, he declined to comment on the scope of Oura’s work with Abu Dhabi. The parallel partnerships come as Ultrahuman and Oura compete in the smart-ring market while looking to push their businesses beyond wearable hardware and deeper into preventive health. The work with the Abu Dhabi authorities also comes as Ultrahuman is looking to build a smart ring that can run software on the device and even power everything from AI interactions to games. Republicans Denounce Trump’s Diesel Deal With Russia’s Putin Amid Mounting Backlash

Update your preferences in Account Settings President Donald Trump is facing mounting calls from within his own party to reverse course on his plan to import diesel from Russia in an effort to stabilize global energy markets, with lawmakers arguing it goes against a 2022 law which banned Russian oil imports. “Congress explicitly banned Russian energy imports in 2022 to prevent American dollars from financing Putin’s brutal war against Ukraine. Congress was clear: The U.S. should not import oil from Russia until it ends its aggression against Ukraine and ceases threats against our NATO allies,” read a joint bipartisan statement released Sunday by four Republican Senators and three Democrats. The lawmakers argued that Trump’s decision to allow Russian diesel imports to the U.S. “runs directly counter to that law” and criticized the lack of congressional overview, insisting that the Administration had failed to consult with Congress or submit any explanation over its decision. “The United States should not provide [Russian President Vladimir] Putin with a financial lifeline while Russia continues to kill Ukrainian civilians. The Administration must immediately reverse course,” they urged. The statement was co-signed by GOP Senators Lisa Murkowski of Alaska, Susan Collins of ⁠Maine, Thom Tillis of North Carolina, and John Curtis of Utah, alongside Democratic Senators Jeanne Shaheen of New Hampshire, Ron Wyden ​of Oregon, and Richard Blumenthal of Connecticut. Trump said Friday he had reached an agreement with Putin for Moscow to "immediately" supply 300,000 tons of diesel to American and global markets. The move comes amid a renewed push by the Administration to lower energy prices for Americans, which have skyrocketed since the Iran war began in February. The increased diesel fuel prices have shaken the loyalties of some of Trump’s most ardent support groups, with farmers telling TIME their day-to-day operations are being severely impacted. In the wake of the diesel supply deal, Trump on Sunday claimed Russia and Ukraine had “agreed” to an energy cease-fire “effective immediately.” However, Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky on Monday said Russian strikes overnight—which reportedly killed ​several people and wounded dozens—had included attacks on energy facilities. “So there were Russian violations of the energy cease-fire announced by President Trump,” said Zelensky. “For now, this is Putin’s answer to Trump—Russian attacks. But we are waiting. We are waiting for the details of the conversation between the Americans and the Russians. We are waiting to see what specific steps the Americans are ready to take.” Trump over the weekend responded to Zelensky's initial criticism of the diesel deal by telling reporters "it's time for Ukraine to get a new President." U.S. lawmakers pushing back against Trump’s Russia diesel plan had sternly warned against putting trust in Putin. Sen. Tillis, during an appearance on NBC’s Meet the Press, expressed concern that the diesel deal could place American lives at risk. “These are bad people hanging out with bad people, putting American lives at risk—particularly our Special Operators and forward-deployed personnel,” Tillis said, insisting that Putin “cannot be trusted.” He also doubled down on his argument that the President’s deal may violate the law passed by Congress four years ago, as it outright banned Russian oil imports unless specific criteria were met, including a full Russian withdrawal from Ukraine. “I do believe there’s a legitimate case for saying the President’s not following the law,” Tillis said. “We were very clear that Russia is the aggressor. Russia is the one who can end this war by backing out of [the] territory they occupied.” Just three weeks ago, Trump signed a sweeping Russia sanctions bill, intended to target the country's financial institutions and the energy industry, which has largely financed the assault on Ukraine. The bill was named after Sen. Lindsey O. Graham, the late Republican Senator who spent the final year of his life building support for the measure. Some Republican lawmakers mentioned this legislation as they criticized the diesel plan. “The President should be using those authorities instead of allowing Russia to export diesel, rake in revenues, and continue its catastrophic war in Ukraine,” said Murkowski. “I oppose President Trump’s decision to lift sanctions on the purchase of diesel from Russia,” said Collins. “I was a co-sponsor of the Russian sanctions law, which the President recently signed, but is now not following.” TIME has reached out to the White House for comment. Republican Rep. Brian Fitzpatrick of Pennsylvania announced Saturday he plans to introduce a bipartisan bill intended to stop Trump from lifting sanctions on Russian oil. “We will be introducing bipartisan legislation to block any and all purchases of Russian oil. It will be called the ‘Ronald Reagan Peace Through Strength Act,’” said Fitzpatrick. Rep. Don Bacon of Nebraska, a Republican who has clashed with Trump on other high-profile issues, said he has already agreed to be a co-sponsor of the bill, insisting that “Congress must make a stand.” Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent has defended the move, insisting the agreement with Russia will work through existing legal channels the U.S. has to help bring prices down. “We’re doing it with all the legal authorities we have, so anyone in Congress who says that—whether it’s an old bill or the current Lindsey Graham bill—prohibits us from doing it, is wrong,” he told Fox News. Republican Sen. John Barrasso ​of Wyoming has also defended the diesel deal, telling CBS' Face the Nation that the President “​has to make tough ⁠decisions.” Zelensky described Trump's initial announcement as a “gift to Putin” and said easing sanctions on Russia was “an obvious weakness” that “plays into Russia’s hands—allowing it to kill more, wage war for longer, have even less respect for America, and inflict even greater losses and damage on the world.” The agreement has also drawn criticism from international leaders, particularly in Europe, where governments have played a central role in supporting Ukraine's defense against Russia. Swedish Prime Minister Ulf Kristersson expressed his “regret” over the U.S. agreement with Russia, warning that “energy revenues are central to the Russian war chest.” Estonian Prime Minister Kristen Michal similarly said the agreement was a “big miscalculation,” arguing that it “is opposite to the long-term activities of the western world to limit the flow of energy carriers to Putin.” Both Sweden and Estonia were among the top 10 countries providing aid to Ukraine as a share of their GDP between January 2022 and February 2026. Kaja Kallas, the E.U.'s foreign policy chief, said that “suspending sanctions on Russian diesel provides Moscow with more revenues to wage war.” On Monday, Kallas announced that European foreign ministers had adopted nearly 1,600 new sanctions targeting Russia's military-industrial complex, underscoring the contrast between Washington's decision and Europe's continued push to economically isolate Moscow. The U.K. has also opposed Trump's agreement. Defense Secretary Wes Streeting said Sunday that Britain did not support the deal with Putin and would instead look to "strengthen sanctions” against Russia. Prime Minister Andy Burnham acknowledged that “the last 24 hours had been difficult for Ukraine and reiterated his complete solidarity” during a phone call with Zelensky. Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney also spoke with Zelensky on Sunday, and expressed his view that the “agreement is in direct opposition to the efforts of Ukraine, Canada, and our allies to create peace and stability in Europe.”
