How Trump’s Policies Are Testing Farmers’ Loyalty Ahead of Midterms
2026-10-12

How Trump’s Policies Are Testing Farmers’ Loyalty Ahead of Midterms

TIME | 2026-10-12

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings U.S. President Donald Trump speaks during an event with American farmers at the White House on June 25, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Rising diesel and fertilizer costs, coupled with President Donald Trump’s shifting tariff policies, have made it harder for Oklahoma farmer Brent Rendel to plan future crops. Over the past few months, he says, the political mood among fellow farmers—traditionally some of the President’s most reliable supporters—has begun to shift. “From my discussions with other farmers, there is a higher level of dissatisfaction with what's going on with the [Trump] Administration,” says Rendel. “You didn't used to really see that, but now we're seeing it and hearing it.” That dissatisfaction could pose a political problem for Trump and Republicans ahead of the pivotal midterm elections. Trump won 69% of the rural vote in 2024, but an AP-KFF poll, conducted in August, found that 57% of rural voters disapprove of Trump’s handling of the economy. Among rural Republican voters, around one-quarter (27%) disapproved of his handling of the economy—and that group was "substantially less likely" than those who approved to say they were certain to vote in November. Three-quarters of rural voters described the cost of living in their communities as “fair” or “poor.” A Reuters/Ipsos poll, conducted in June, had already put Trump's approval rating among rural Americans dropped at a new low of 50%, with just 31% approving of his handling of economic issues. The findings come as Democrats poll competitively in some traditionally Republican states such as Texas, Iowa, and Kansas. Democratic leaders are also addressing farmers' economic frustrations in their policy proposals. Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer and Sen. Amy Klobuchar, ranking member of the Senate Agriculture Committee, last month unveiled a bill intended to lay the groundwork for “America’s first-ever fertilizer reserve.” American farmers are “getting crushed from all sides by this Administration,” Schumer said as he introduced the Strategic Fertilizer Reserve Act with endorsements from the National Farmers Union and New York Farm Bureau. Farmers and agricultural experts interviewed by TIME described four overlapping pressures: Trump’s expansion of low-tariff beef imports, rising fertilizer and diesel costs stemming from a global energy crisis triggered by the Iran war, and tariffs that have increased some production costs while provoking retaliation against American exports. The impact has been felt by consumers, who have seen a steady climb in grocery prices. In one September AP-NORC poll, 64% of Americans said Trump has “gone too far” on imposing new tariffs on other countries. Trump’s latest tariff row with Canada has prompted further economic concern, particularly in rural states such as Montana, which listed Canada as its largest export market in 2025. In response, White House spokesperson Olivia Wales told TIME that Trump “has repeatedly affirmed his support for America’s great farmers and ranchers.” She added: “While the President has been clear about temporary disruptions as a result of the conflict in Iran, the Administration continues to implement the proven Trump agenda of tax cuts, deregulation, and energy abundance to keep America on a solid economic trajectory.” Trump campaigned on promises to keep the U.S. out of foreign wars and focus on prosperity at home. The farmers who spoke to TIME say the economic consequences of his policies are now reaching their day-to-day operations. President Donald Trump walks past tractors as he arrives to participate in a roundtable on "American Agriculture" at Custer Farms in Chippewa Falls, Wisconsin, on June 5, 2026. Saul Loeb—Getty Images The average price of ground beef in the U.S. has risen sharply since the end of 2020. It climbed from $5.58 per pound in August 2024 to $6.92 in August 2026, according to U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics data. Shrinking cattle herd sizes, drought and strong demand have all contributed to the increases. At the start of this year, the U.S. cattle inventory hit a 75-year low. The price surge has created a political challenge for Trump. “A major part of [Trump’s] campaign to households around the U.S. two years ago, was the fact that they could keep inflation low,” says Joe Glauber, a research fellow at the International Food Policy Research Institute, noting that many rural Americans and farmers are concerned that this hasn’t materialized. Trump’s 90-day scheme to suspend tariffs on 300,000 metric tons of imported foreign beef to ease the strain on grocery bills went into effect on Sept. 1. The stated intention was for the imported beef to be sold at 25% lower than market price. But the American Farm Bureau Federation (AFBF), an agriculture lobbying group, has urged Trump to reverse course, citing its own analysis that found the increased imports have not lowered prices for consumers. The AFBF tracked daily prices of 80% lean ground beef at 41 grocery stores in 22 states, starting the day after the proclamation went into effect. In 30 of those 41 stores, ground beef prices cost relatively the same on Sep. 23 as they did on Sep. 2. Incumbent Texas Commissioner for Agriculture Sid Miller, an eighth-generation farmer and rancher, says farmers have felt the effects of the fallout of the beef import scheme. “I haven't talked to one rancher that's not upset about it,” says Miller. “Just the packers and the processors are reaping the benefits, not the consumer or the cattle rancher. Calves that are going to market now are bringing $300-500 a head less.” Derrell Peel, a professor of agricultural economics at Oklahoma State University who specializes in livestock and cattle markets, says ground beef has become the “poster child for higher food prices,” making it a focus of the political debate. “Cattle producers increasingly feel that Trump is willing to throw them under the bus to try to get lower beef prices before the election,” Peel tells TIME. “None of the political actions have had any significant impact on beef prices and there is no quick solution to high beef prices.” In Florida, Kansas, Kentucky, Missouri, Montana, Nebraska, North Dakota, Oklahoma, South Dakota, and Texas—10 major beef-producing states—Trump has seen a decline in approval rating, according to Civiqs polling data collected between Aug. 21-Sep. 21. “For Republicans, as a rule, our strongest voting bloc is the rural areas of the United States,” says Miller. “I think we're going to lose some statewide races in Texas because of multiple things,” he adds, listing tariffs, diesel fuel costs, and fertilizer prices as other key concerns. “It's like death from 1000 cuts.” Recent ICE raids targeting meatpacking plants across Kansas, Oklahoma, and Texas are also causing concern and placing further strain on the U.S. beef supply chain. “ICE [recently] raided workers from packing plants in Kansas, shutting the packing plants down,” says Miller. “That lowers prices for the cattlemen because the cattle back up, they can't get processed, and it raises prices for the consumer, because there's less beef on the market.” Homeland Security Secretary Markwayne Mullin on Sept. 25 disputed the nature of the raids and said “ICE is not conducting worksite operations in Kansas.” Industry groups said worker absences nevertheless forced some plants to close temporarily. Supporters of President Donald Trump carry lawn signs at the Westmoreland Fair fair in Pennsylvania on Aug. 21, 2024. Andrew Lichtenstein—Getty Images Steve Sheffels, a wheat farmer who serves as president of the Montana Grain Growers Association, says that around 40% of his expenses go towards buying fertilizer. He says that outlook could become “dire” if prices continue to rise. Rising fertilizer costs, combined with extreme weather in Montana over the past year, has led Sheffels to conclude that “we are not able to produce our crops and make money doing it.” Sheffels highlights a specific example of how his costs have risen since the onset of the Iran war. “We just got done seeding this fall, and the cost of starter fertilizer, which has a lot of potassium in it, was just under $1,000 a ton,” he says. “Last year, it was $600 a ton.” The rise of fertilizer costs is a major concern as crops such as corn and wheat heavily rely on nitrogen fertilizer. Natural gas is key in the production of nitrogen-based fertilizers, including urea and anhydrous ammonia. Exports of liquified natural gas from Gulf countries have been severely disrupted by the war and shipping disruptions have continued. The shipping of natural gas exports via the Strait of Hormuz “remained effectively suspended” through August, according to data published last month, while other fertilizer product exports “remained sporadic and subdued” across the month. In April, 94% of farmers responding to a Farmer & Rancher Policy Sentiment survey said the Iran war had impacted their business by raising fertilizer costs, energy costs, or both. While the survey came amid a sharp spike in fertilizer prices across April to June, concerns remain. The price of anhydrous ammonia was up 9.3% as of late September compared to late February, before the onset of the war. The price of urea is up 7.9% for the same period, according to data from analytics company DTN. Workers spread fertilizer after planting potatoes at Bluff View Farms in West Jefferson, North Carolina, on April 24, 2026. Allison Joyce—Getty Images In the U.S., the price of diesel hit record highs last month, sitting at a national average of over $6.52 per gallon on Sept. 22, according to the American Automobile Association. Trump signed an Executive Order last week to waive the off-road requirement for a specific type of tax-free diesel fuel primarily used by the agriculture industry. Temporarily allowing highway use of red-dyed diesel “will put money directly back in the pockets of American farmers, truckers, and workers,” the White House said. While it may help lessen the costs associated with the transportation of harvested goods, Rendel says it “won’t be the solution for our farm economy woes.” Days later, the President also announced that Russia ‌had agreed to supply over 300,000 tons ​of diesel fuel ​to the American and ⁠global marketplace. Prior to these efforts, Trump floated the possibility of a diesel export ban. But he ruled out the idea on Oct. 2 as the G7 group of nations committed to a co-ordinated release ‌of 100 million barrels of diesel alongside other reserves through the International Energy Agency (IEA), in an effort to stabilise world energy markets. In the meantime, farmers continue to grapple with increased prices. “Diesel directly affects farm production costs for field operations, planting, harvesting, and for shipping both inputs and outputs,” says Peel. “The impact [when prices are increased] is very immediate.” In the August AP/KFF poll, 67% of rural voters said they were “very or somewhat” worried about gasoline or other transportation costs. Rendel is already feeling the impact. Last month, he explains, he had diesel delivered to his farm that cost him over $5 a gallon. “I know in a lot of the world, that's probably not ridiculous, but for farm diesel, which is subjected to a lower tax rate in the U.S., that is ridiculous,” he says. “Anytime it goes over $4, it gets painful, and now we're already over five.” “Fuel is a real strong pain point for everyone. As long as the war is going on, there's really not going to be any significant relief available,” he adds. Crop farmers in particular rely on heavy, diesel-fueled machinery to sow and harvest. “If fertilizer prices rise, which they have risen over the past year or so, you can really look at your soil fertility and make decisions that can save you some money. When seed prices go up, you can shop around and maybe find less expensive seed or reduce your seeding rates,” Rendel says. “But fuel's not that way. My combine, when I'm harvesting soybeans, uses about 200 gallons of fuel per day, and that doesn't change.” Diesel is also essential for transporting goods once they are harvested. “I’ve got to deliver the grain to the buyer. The fuel is a very inflexible expense,” Rendel says, citing a lack of alternatives. Sheffels is feeling the impact of transport costs as a result of diesel fuel prices, too. “We're having to pay more to get our grain moved around in the short term,” he says. “In the long term, it's going to have a huge [financial] impact because of the distances.” The Agriculture Department's display at the Great American State Fair in Washington, D.C., on June 25, 2026. Jim West/Universal Images Group—Getty Images Trump has imposed rounds of tariffs since returning to the White House, prompting retaliatory measures from countries including China and Canada. The renewed trade war with Canada has added to concerns for farmers in the U.S., after negotiations between the two countries collapsed in August. Canada’s retaliatory tariffs on U.S. goods took effect last month, with Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney matching the levies imposed by the Trump Administration. Canada's counter tariffs apply to products covering $27.6 billion in imports from the U.S., focusing on sectors such as steel, dairy, appliances, agricultural equipment, pulp and paper, among other products. “The U.S. and Canadian economies are highly integrated,” says Peel. “There are no winners in trade wars in general and especially in this one with so many components that will be impacted.” Overall, tariffs on key production inputs have imposed roughly $2.6 billion in additional costs on crop farmers from February 2025 through July 2026, according to data from the Association of Equipment Manufacturers. Following Chinese President Xi Jinping’s visit to the White House in late September, Beijing agreed to drop tariffs on various U.S. agricultural products. However, soybeans were excluded from the list, and will remain subjected to 10% tariffs, offering little relief to farmers like Rendel. Soybeans remained the top U.S. agricultural export to China in 2025, even as shipments fell by roughly three-quarters between 2024 and 2025 amid Trump’s implementation of wide ranging tariffs on Beijing. For farmers already facing higher diesel and fertilizer costs, shrinking export markets create pressure at both ends of the supply chain. “You're hitting people at both levels, at the input level, and then look at the prices they get for their commodity if they're not able to export it,” says Glauber, from the International Food Policy Research Institute. Sheffels says the repeated policy shifts are disruptive for farmers across America. “The level of uncertainty that we've had to live with is completely unacceptable,” he says. “It's impossible to make investment decisions when you don't know what the rules are going to be five minutes from now, let alone in six months.” With the midterms fast approaching, the question for Republicans is whether that frustration will change how some farmers vote—or whether they vote at all.

What to Know About the Controversy Surrounding Kimberly Guilfoyle

What to Know About the Controversy Surrounding Kimberly Guilfoyle

TIME | 2026-10-12

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings U.S. Ambassador to Greece Kimberly Guilfoyle speaks during a dedication ceremony at the chancery of the U.S. Embassy in Athens, on Oct. 7, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings President Donald Trump is reportedly considering dismissing the U.S. ambassador to Greece, former Fox News host and ex-fiancée of Donald Trump Jr. Kimberly Guilfoyle, following reports of alleged misconduct before and during her tenure in Athens. The Associated Press and CNN, citing unnamed sources, said that a decision on Guilfoyle is expected soon. A senior U.S. official told the AP on Sunday that Trump and Secretary of State Marco Rubio are “extremely pissed off” by Guilfoyle’s reported conduct and would likely assess future action after the Monday U.S. holiday. The controversy emerged in late September, when the Wall Street Journal began publishing a series of reports about Guilfoyle’s questionable activities—including promoting a Greek construction firm, bragging about luxury trips paid for by businessmen, and asking a Trump donor to pay a $100,000 credit card bill in exchange for getting the attention of the Trump Administration. The State Department’s inspector general is looking into the allegations, the AP and the Journal reported. TIME has reached out to the White House and the State Department for comment, as well as a lawyer for Guilfoyle. The reports surfaced around Rubio’s multiple-day visit to Europe last week, when the Secretary of State boasted about the Trump Administration’s achievements and Guilfoyle attended most of his events in Athens. When asked about the reports on Oct. 8, Rubio at first appeared incredulous, telling journalists: “I don’t even know if what you’re reporting is true. You guys somehow walk around thinking that because some source told you something and you reported it, it’s the gospel. It’s not the gospel. It’s not the gospel for me.” As the reports emerged, Trump did not defend Guilfoyle, who was in a relationship with his eldest son between 2018 and 2024. “I just heard about it—too bad,” the President told reporters on Friday. “I mean, I don’t know. She’s an ambassador. I just heard a little bit about it. I don’t know too much. She was always very nice.” Vice President J.D. Vance reserved comment on the reporting around Guilfoyle, though he said Thursday that he does not like “anybody selling access to another government official, certainly a high-level government official,” and added the next day that “if there was something bad that happened, then people have to suffer consequences for it.” Trump announced he would nominate Guilfoyle to be the U.S. ambassador to Greece in December 2024, just before the start of his second term and amid reports of her split with Trump Jr. She was sworn in in September 2025, becoming the first woman in the role. The 57-year-old Guilfoyle was a former prosecutor in the San Francisco and Los Angeles District Attorney’s Offices and was appointed Assistant District Attorney in San Francisco in 2000. She gained fame as a co-host of the Fox News’ conservative political talk show The Five, which she joined in 2011 and left in 2018. Besides Guilfoyle’s legal and media work, she also helped raise funds for Trump’s presidential campaign in 2020. Guilfoyle was also a witness to the events leading up to the Jan. 6, 2021 U.S. Capitol attack, and met with the House committee investigating the insurrection in 2022. From 2001 to 2006, Guilfoyle was married to Gavin Newsom, then a San Francisco supervisor and later mayor and now California’s governor who’s widely seen as a Democratic presidential contender. On Sept. 25, the Journal published a report on Guilfoyle promoting the Greek construction firm Aktor Group and having the company’s lobbyist, Christos Marafatsos, attend diplomatic meetings across Balkan states. Guilfoyle reportedly encouraged governments from Bulgaria to Albania to work with Aktor Group. The Sept. 25 report also detailed Guilfoyle’s alleged behavior with Greek government officials. According to the Journal report, during a March dinner, Guilfoyle told attendees, including the country’s energy minister, that the U.S. could influence political changes in other countries, which the minister balked at. Per the Journal, Guilfoyle’s lawyer disputed how the dinner unfolded. The Greek government also denied the threat, adding: “Such a thing would never be tolerated.” Marafatsos’s lawyer also contested the Journal’s reporting. Lawyers for Aktor Group denied allegations that Guilfoyle acted improperly in promoting its business interests. Then, on Oct. 7, the Journal reported, citing Guilfoyle’s text messages ahead of her Senate confirmation hearing in July 2025, that she asked businessman and Trump donor Eric Deters to wire $100,000 to clear her American Express credit card debt in exchange for access to the Trump Administration. Deters, who did not pay Guilfoyle, told the paper that he had transactions with Guilfoyle dating back to 2020. The Journal reported that Deters promised Guilfoyle as much $1 million if she succeeded in helping him connect with the Trump Administration, and that she reportedly offered to use her links as Trump Jr.’s former fiancée. Guilfoyle also reportedly asked Deters to stop creating a paper trail of their dealings. Jesse Binnall, Guilfoyle’s lawyer, told the Journal that the ambassador disputes the messages’ authenticity. He also said that Guilfoyle was paid for events before her nomination and that the ambassador “was owed a balance for traveling to and supporting these patriotic events.” Three days later, the Journal published another report, this time on how Guilfoyle flaunted luxury trips financed by several businessmen, through an audio recording that the paper reviewed. She reportedly acknowledged in the recording that she flew to the January 2026 World Economic Forum on a private jet with Aktor Group’s CEO Alexandros Exarchou. Guilfoyle also reportedly said in the recording that she planned to travel with a Greek shipping magnate, who reportedly also paid for her accommodations. Responding to the Journal’s latest report, a spokesperson for the U.S. Embassy in Athens said that Guilfoyle has “faithfully followed State Department ethics and gift reporting guidelines.” Guilfoyle and the allegations surrounding her add to other issues hounding the Trump Administration heading into the November midterms, where Republicans are hoping to defend their majorities in Congress. Some Democrats have called for her removal. In a Sept. 30 letter to Rubio, Rep. Gregory Meeks (D, N.Y.), the top Democrat on the House Foreign Affairs Committee, sought answers about the allegations. Meeks said in a statement that the reports about Guilfoyle’s actions were “deeply concerning and suggest potentially corrupt influences on U.S. foreign policy,” adding that if found true, she should be “removed from her position.” Rep. Robert Garcia (D, Calif.), the top Democrat on the House Oversight Committee, wrote to Guilfoyle on Oct. 8 that her reported conduct with Deters “raises serious public corruption and national security concerns.” Garcia requested she hand over financial and communications records related to the controversy. Even some top Republicans are concerned: GOP Senate Majority Whip John Barrasso of Wyoming called the reports about Guilfoyle “very troubling.” “I’m sure there’s going to be an investigation,” Barrasso told CBS News Sunday. “I know that the Senate Foreign Relations Committee will have oversight and look into that as well. But I think the public has a right to know what is found, and it is quite a bit of concern.”

The Case for Shifting Mental Health Care From Institutions to Communities
2026-10-12

The Case for Shifting Mental Health Care From Institutions to Communities

TIME | 2026-10-12

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings In recent decades, many countries have made meaningful progress in developing community-based mental health services. Moreover, rights-based approaches have gained traction as people with lived experience increasingly have a voice in shaping mental health policy and services. These developments have shown that people living with mental health conditions can flourish in their communities when given the right support. Yet, around the world, very large numbers of people with mental health conditions live behind closed doors in psychiatric hospitals and social care institutions. Some have been there for months; others for decades. According to the World Health Organization’s Mental Health Atlas, about half were brought in against their will, stripped of basic daily choices like what to wear or when to eat, and cut off from the outside world. We think of institutional mental health care as an outdated model. But it is far more than that. It is a human rights issue hiding in plain sight. Many countries still rely heavily on standalone psychiatric institutions. Across the world, we estimate that nearly half of all public mental health funding is spent on maintaining these large facilities, while access to community-based mental health care, including in general hospitals, for millions of people remains underfunded and overlooked. Sri Lanka embraced mental healthcare reform by replacing isolated institutions with community-based services. Guided by the country’s mental health policies over the past 20 years, Sri Lanka has made significant progress in transforming the mental health system from an asylum-based model to a decentralized network of district and community services. Today, mental health care in Sri Lanka is delivered through inpatient and outpatient units in general hospitals, outreach clinics, intermediate rehabilitation units, and child mental health clinics throughout the country. Facilities that uphold human rights, dignity, and personal autonomy promote recovery. However, when care is characterized by coercion, rigid control, or social isolation, it risks becoming a form of confinement rather than care. Deinstitutionalization is not simply about reducing hospital beds. It is about creating the conditions for people to live independently and participate fully in society, with access to appropriate housing, education, employment, social protection, and person-centered mental and physical health care. This is the foundation of recovery, inclusion and human rights-based mental health care. Scaling back psychiatric hospital beds must go hand in hand with building up local services and practical supports—housing, community mental health care, peer support, social connection, education and employment, legal support, and support for families. No one should be discharged without continuity of care. People should be welcomed into services and communities that are ready for them. Countries worldwide are proving that advancing deinstitutionalization is achievable across all income levels, from changing care practices and expanding alternatives to psychiatric hospitals to establishing comprehensive networks of community-based services. Deinstitutionalization is a cultural shift that requires the meaningful involvement of people with lived experience, health service providers, and the whole of society. To support this transition, the World Health Organization has released new, practical guidance to help health leaders navigate each step. Every country begins from a different position, with different resources, laws, service systems and community networks. Implementers need to identify feasible, high-impact actions that are appropriate to their own context. We must ensure that people receive the support they need in environments that promote dignity, autonomy, relationships and inclusion.  We call on governments, health and social care systems to make this a priority, with people with lived experience as partners throughout the transition. We Need to Bring Back Matchmaking Our Friends
2026-10-12

We Need to Bring Back Matchmaking Our Friends

TIME | 2026-10-12

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Last year, I started hosting monthly house parties. When I saw two friends get on really well, I set them up on a date. “You two should go out sometime,” I casually said. “I think you’d have a lot in common.” So far, there have been three dates among people who met at my house parties. Little did I know, my unintentional matchmaking efforts may have a higher success rate than dating apps. In my view, one sad side effect of the rise of dating apps has been a noticeable decline in matchmaking. Whatever happened to setting friends up on a blind date? Or even just recommending that two people you love give it a shot of finding love themselves? And in our modern moment of isolation, polarization, and loneliness, it is clear to me that now is a crucial time for us to bring back matchmaking for our friends. When one of my friends called me after her date to get more insight about my other friend, I was able to answer all her concerns. That couldn’t have happened if she had met a stranger on a dating app. Dating apps have made meeting someone feel increasingly detached from the people and communities we already belong to. We swipe through strangers based on a handful of photographs and prompts, often with very little context about who they are beyond what they have chosen to present. A friend, on the other hand, can tell you things an algorithm never could: what someone is actually like, how they treat people, whether they are kind, funny, or reliable, and whether they are compatible with you. “Friends, especially long-term friends, know literally every nuance of who we are that an algorithm or app cannot capture. Digital mechanisms capture factual data,” says Blythe Landry, a trauma and relationship therapist. “Friends carry history, trauma narratives, inside jokes, the way our nose wrinkles when we laugh, and our favorite order at Starbucks.” Our nearest and dearest know our likes from the inside out and, perhaps most importantly, they know we can sometimes be totally blind to our patterns in choosing romantic partners. Many of us have the experience a year after a breakup when a best friend says, “I knew that was never going to work! I just didn’t say anything because it was important for you to see that for yourself.” Friends also know our limitations and what we can and cannot bring to a partner. They are more likely to be brutally honest with us than an app that matches us with someone in our area. Once, I had to tell a friend of mine that the person she met at my house party and went on a date with might not be compatible with her financially. Ultimately, she agreed. Too often, we underestimate the value of this kind of social knowledge. I remember being annoyed at my friend when she casually told me that she has three brothers. I am not saying I wanted to get with any of the brothers, but surely those brothers have big friend groups and she could have set me up with someone? My friend herself has been in a relationship over the last few years and had no idea that I would prefer her help over the multiple dating app options I have at my disposal. “Why would you want to meet any of my stupid brothers’ friends when you have the whole world at your fingertips?” She asked me innocently. When we erase community and friendships from the dating experience, dating becomes strategic in the most unpleasant of ways, says Landry. “Because we are now in a time where every single thing we do is online, there are simply way too many ways to ‘not engage’ with someone online, even if offline they could be our perfect match.” Landry’s best friend died in 2021 of cancer, but years before her death, she had the great pleasure of introducing her to the absolute love of her life. “It was my one and only setup, and it was literally like I momentarily became a professional matchmaker.” Both of her friends had been single for a decade. “One day, I was at coffee with my friend, and he ordered his tea the exact same way my other friend ordered hers.” At that moment, time stood still and out of her mouth came, “You should really meet my friend.” They both protested. Both told Landry that they had nothing in common and had no interest in the other. Just weeks later, they were living together. “While I will always miss my friend, I will forever be so happy she got to find true love with someone,” she says. “And, point being, no app could have ever caught that ‘match’ moment like a best friend could.” There is something reassuring about being set up by someone who knows both people involved. It also changes the stakes of dating: you aren’t meeting a complete stranger who exists entirely outside your social world, but someone who already has a connection to it. Perhaps this is why we also need to bring back house parties, hosting a casual dinner, and planned drinks with friends. But no matter the form, this is why we need to bring back matchmaking. When dating is mediated by apps, we have fewer opportunities to organically meet the friends of our friends. House parties create the kind of low-pressure social environment where those connections can happen without anyone having to announce that they are “looking for love.” My three successful matches are obviously not scientific evidence that dinner parties can replace dating apps. But they have made me wonder whether we have outsourced too much of our romantic lives to technology and whether our friends, who already know us better than any algorithm, should have a bigger role in helping us find someone. The return of matchmaking among friends could offer an antidote to the impersonal nature of app dating. I believe that the energy you put out there is the energy you get back, so I am hoping that while I play cupid for my friends, they too, at some point in the future, will decide to set me up with someone suitable. Opinions expressed do not necessarily reflect the views of TIME editors.

‘Ocean of the Americas’: Trump Floats Renaming Atlantic or Pacific
2026-10-12

‘Ocean of the Americas’: Trump Floats Renaming Atlantic or Pacific

TIME | 2026-10-12

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings President Donald Trump arrives to deliver remarks during a rally at F&M Bank Arena in Clarksville, Tenn., on Oct. 10, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings After renaming a gulf and a great lake, President Donald Trump now has his sights set on an ocean. At a rally in Clarksville, Tenn., on Saturday evening, Trump suggested the Atlantic or Pacific Ocean could be renamed in the country’s—or his—honor. “Before I get out, I think we should have an ocean,” Trump said. “We call it ‘Trump Ocean’ or ‘Ocean of the Americas,’ something like that.” The two oceans are international bodies of water that span many countries’ jurisdictions as well as the high seas. The President has executive authority to direct U.S. geographic naming authorities to adopt different names for federal government use, which would make those names the standard across federal agencies. Other countries and private companies would not be required to follow suit, although private mapping services including Google Maps and Apple Maps have previously changed their official labels for certain geographic features for U.S. users in accordance with Trump’s orders. Trump also said that “we need a great river” to rename, but he ruled out either the Hudson River or the Mississippi River. “He did a good job of sailing up that sucker a long time ago,” Trump said, referring to explorer Henry Hudson, for whom the river running through Trump’s home state of New York was named. Trump said he would leave the Mississippi River’s name alone because of support for him in the state. “I love Mississippi. We won that state by so much,” he said. Trump appeared at the rally to campaign for Sen. Marsha Blackburn, the Republican candidate for Tennessee governor, and Sen. Bill Hagerty, who is seeking reelection in the November midterm elections. Turning to the Senate race in Michigan, one of several states that  has seen a progressive victory in its Democratic primary, Trump called the Democratic nominee Abdul El-Sayed, who is not a member of the Democratic Socialists of America, “Mohammed.” Read More: Socialism is More Popular Than Ever in the U.S., According to Polls “Somebody said, ‘His name isn’t Mohammed. It’s Abdul,’” Trump said. “I’m the President. I can call him whatever the hell I want.” Trump has shown a fondness for renaming geographic features. In January 2025, he signed an executive order directing federal agencies to use “Gulf of America” in place of the Gulf of Mexico and restoring “Mount McKinley” as the federal name for Alaska’s highest peak, also known as Denali. The renaming of the gulf set off a dispute with Mexico, which argued the U.S. had no authority to rename the entire body of water, part of which lies within Mexico’s jurisdiction. Mexico later sued Google over its use of “Gulf of America” for U.S. users. “Everyone thinks I kid,” Trump said Saturday about his original proposal to rename the gulf. “Everybody smiled, ‘Oh, that’s so funny.’ I did it, right? It’s done.” In April, Trump joked that he had considered calling it the “Gulf of Trump.” In August, Trump signed another order directing federal agencies to call Lake Ontario, which borders New York and the Canadian province of Ontario, “Lake America.” The name change came amid an escalating U.S.-Canada tariff war and prompted rebuke from Democratic lawmakers as well as Canadian officials: Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney rejected the new name, saying Canadians would call it Lake Ontario “then, now and always.” Last month, Trump floated renaming the state of New Mexico as “New America.” He used the name again in an Oct. 3 video endorsing Republican Greg Cunningham for the state’s 2nd congressional district. But the President said that the state’s name would be left “up to” New Mexicans. “I’m not going to push it, but a lot of people are liking it a lot,” he said. Officially renaming a state would likely require approval by the state and Congress. Also in September, Trump suggested renaming the Strait of Hormuz, the waterway over which fighting has intensified amid the U.S. and Israel’s war against Iran, the “Trump Strait.” When asked how serious he was, Trump said the idea was “just thrown out there.” He reiterated the idea at Saturday’s rally, saying “we’re thinking about doing it,” but he said he wouldn’t make the name change himself. Amid raucous applause on Saturday, the President mused about the affection he receives at rallies. He told the crowd,  “I could say the stupidest things and you’d clap.”

Q3 2026 Was Historically Bad For PC Shipments

and in more locked down env this will lead to something failing. Let's Encrypt's mission was to get everyone to use SSL... OK. Nice. Thank you. But now it's "We're reducing the validity window to force you to use automation by making manual updating more frequent than people will tolerate". What the hell is so bad about manual updates? There's a whole world of people out there who are too small to set up a nice automated system but who still should be using SSL, and this is Let's Encrypt telling us they don't really believe in their mission statement. Sounds like theres a need for an easy automation solution. The problem isn't the automation, it's when the automation fails. Do you remember how to fix the certificate manually? Or do you rely on the automated tool to never fail, and it just did? It's easy if it's regularly practiced or someone documented the steps, but it's rarely going to be practiced (likely forgotten if the automation works for years) and documentation gets lost. It might be in a great spot in the IT wiki and IT documentation, but that gets forgotten about as platforms migrate. People also leave. Suddenly, the automation fails. Maybe it got a bad certificate from the CA. Or maybe a parameter changed that triggered some unforseen bug in the automation. or an update was run that had a bug when used in the environment. Now it fails. And you're screwed, because no one knows how to fix it. Automation works, until it doesn't. Then things that were relying on it break. Maybe you're lucky and you catch things breaking early to realize what is happening. But chances are, one day you'll try to log in, and you'll get a big "Certificate Expired" screen on the browser and suddenly your VM clusters are inaccessible because they don't have valid certs (seen this happen with openvirt) and the company is at a standstill becauze nothing works. The danger of automation isn't when it works. It's when it doesn't and no human has touched the system in years because of it and tribal knowledge has dissipated in the winds, documentation is lost, and no one remembers how the system worked. The trouble with really short expiries is you have a lot less time to fix issues before everything breaks. With 90 day expiries, you can comfortably renew your certificates two weeks before expiry, giving you plenty of time to sort out any issues before things break. With the 7-day expiries they're pushing for, you've got almost no time between scheduled renewal and certificate expiry to get stuff sorted out when things inevitably go wrong. The point of the really short expiries seems to be to compensate The mission is to get everyone on SSL, that's going to include people without a clue Sure, we'll just assign "master all things x509" to engineer #78 of 205... in our four-person shop.You can't. News and Politics - Slate Magazine
Here’s How—and Where—It Could All Go Wrong for Democrats
2026-10-10

Here’s How—and Where—It Could All Go Wrong for Democrats

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-10

Visit Original Website
The Surge is your weekly political sanity check: What matters, what might, and what’s just political theater. Sign up here to get it in your inbox. Welcome to this week’s edition of the Surge, a politics newsletter that was glad to be reminded that we need to find a rich person to pay off our $100,000 in credit card debt if we’re ever going to get that ambassadorship. So … any takers? The air had a crisp autumnal smell of politics to it this week. The Surge has pulled its giant foam finger saying “DEBATES” out of storage, because friends, it is debate season. Wonder what Ken Paxton thinks privately about anything? You’ll probably learn about it through leaked audio soon. We take a look at the looming Senate Democratic leadership race (there won’t be one) and observe Trump taking a warning shot at two of his top political advisers. Let’s begin, though, with a look at the Senate map, and the state it might all come down to. In January, after Democratic former Rep. Mary Peltola announced she’d run for Senate in Alaska, Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer gave a series of interviews about how he’d landed “the last piece of the puzzle” toward retaking the Senate majority. The path he laid out ran through Alaska, Ohio, North Carolina, and Maine. “We couldn’t have better candidates,” he told Semafor. While some new opportunities for Senate Democrats have since entered the fray—Iowa, Texas, and Kansas—it’s still those four states that represent the core pathway. And by our reckoning, it’s the bluest of those four states, Maine, that represents Democrats’ biggest hurdle. Republicans at this point have effectively ceded the North Carolina Senate race to Democrat Roy Cooper, with both the Senate Republicans’ super PAC and GOP candidate Michael Whatley’s campaign itself pausing their advertising. (Note: Cooper does still have to win more real-life votes than Whatley in the election for this concession to become official.) Alaska and Ohio are hardly in Democrats’ column already. But Ohio Democratic Senate candidate Sherrod Brown has consistently posted polling leads against a weak opponent in Sen. Jon Husted. And just this week, Sabato’s Crystal Ball, one of the top election forecasters, shifted Alaska from a “toss-up” race to “leans Democratic.” Which brings us to Maine. Oh, Maine. RealClearPolitics’ polling average shows Democratic candidate Troy Jackson with a 1-point lead over Sen. Susan Collins—but with significant variation between individual polls, and in a state where Collins tends to outperform her polls. The list of reasons why Democrats should take this seat without much struggle is limitless. Briefly, though: It’s a blue state in a blue year where the Republican president is heinously unpopular. Trump has picked a stupid trade war with a country abutting Maine, with which it conducts significant amounts of trade for daily goods. Mainers need heating oil, and heating oil costs are through the roof. But there are two reasons why the race is on a knife’s edge. The first is that Jackson is not running against a generic, hapless Republican, he’s running against Susan fucking Collins. The second is the long tail of Democrats’ candidate drama. When Schumer gave those interviews in January about how “we couldn’t have better candidates,” he was referring to his personal recruit, Gov. Janet Mills. But, as one member of Graham Platner’s team told us when they were being criticized for not having properly vetted him, Schumer’s vetting of Mills missed that she was 78 years old and didn’t want to run. The rest of the story we’re all familiar with: Platner built a movement, and then imploded personally, leaving Jackson as the warm body to fill the ballot line. As our colleague Ian Prasad Philbrick wrote in an excellent piece about the race this week, “now the party is stuck, trying to win an election Platner shaped with a candidate who has to shoulder much of his predecessor’s baggage but lacks many of his talents.” It would just make too much sense, and be so dang Democratty, to fall just short of Senate control by failing to beat Susan Collins for the 900th time, with the best chance they’ve ever had to do so. Too much sense, too neatly choreographed. So maybe Democrats will get it done after all. Feeling a little under the weather? That’s because you’ve got debate fever, suffering reader. We’re at that point in the calendar where people across the country (well, us, since it’s our job) need a C-SPAN multi-view feature to keep up with all of the jabs; the digs. Do any of these debates matter? As much as we’d love to play it cool and laugh off the notion that anything matters, well, they sometimes do! Republicans lost a Senate seat in Indiana in 2012 after their candidate said women who’d been impregnated via rape should be required to keep the pregnancy. South Carolina Sen. Darline Graham quite nearly lost her primary a couple of months ago after saying she didn’t know anything about foreign policy. And if we’re remembering correctly, a 2024 debate between Joe Biden and Donald Trump had a substantial effect on that race. We didn’t notice any blow-ups of such magnitude in the debates we watched this week. Our main takeaway from watching Senate debates in Georgia, Maine, and Michigan was how they reinforced the state of each race. Sen. Jon Ossoff is cruising to reelection in Georgia against Rep. Mike Collins, and it’s so easy to see why: With perfect poise and discipline, he delivered banger after banger against his opponent. (For example: “If John Lewis were marching today from Selma to Montgomery, Mike Collins would be grabbing a billy club.” The kid’s a killer.) In Maine—a viewing experience that shaped our perspective of this race in the lead entry—Troy Jackson had all of the advantages in the world but struggled to get his answers out clearly, while Collins, always perfectly prepared, was at no point caught off guard. In Michigan, Democrat Abdul El-Sayed was, as ever, perfectly eloquent, charismatic, and cocky, but took repeated hits against some of his more left-wing positions, such as single-payer healthcare. Republican candidate Mike Rogers, meanwhile, consistently race-baited El-Sayed. In an illustrative example, Rogers described his opponent as “bought off by Hamas, Hezbollah, al-Qaida.” But since no one in these debates parsed the meaning of “rape,” declared that they don’t know anything about the world, or fell asleep during their answers—things of that nature—we’re not expecting any major shifts from these. You may have heard chatter about Democratic leadership in the next Senate. You may even have heard murmurings that Chuck Schumer, who lost standing in the party in last year’s government funding battles, might have his work cut out to remain as leader. This is nonsense. So long as Schumer runs for leader again, he will be the leader. If Schumer actually does flip a net of four Senate seats and retake the majority, it would not just ensure he gets another term as leader—it would be his finest political feat, period. If he comes up just short, he’ll be fine, as it’s not like some other leader would have found better recruits in states like Alaska, Texas, Iowa, or Ohio. And if the night goes catastrophically for Democrats and they pick up no seats, or even lose a couple, well, that just lowers the number of senators Schumer would need to win another term—to 23, 24, 25. Schumer does indeed have that many friends within the Senate Democratic caucus. Now, a good question for next year is whether Schumer, 75, chooses to take the opportunity time has presented him to step down after the next Congress, following 12 years as leader, or run for reelection and spend two years dragging the caucus through the messy politics of his own primary. But again: a good question for next year. ·     Trump chastised two of his top outside political hands, Chris LaCivita and James Blair, on social media after a story ran about how they were doing some consulting for a pro-Russian political party in the Balkans, threatening to fire them if he discovers anything untoward. Trump’s reaction makes no sense at first. Since when does he care about conflicts of interest or people in his orbit getting paid by pro-Russian factions? What’s happening here is that Trump put LaCivita and Blair in charge of his midterm operation, the midterm operation is not going well, and he’s mad at them. He’s reminding them who their meal ticket is. ·      In other Trump posting news this week: He has nicknamed South Carolina Democratic Senate candidate Annie Andrews “Annie Trannie.” Yes, oh yes, it is newsworthy that the president of the United States is using this slur. Zooming out, though, it’s even more newsworthy that he’s turning his attention to Andrews at all. He posted the slur while reiterating his endorsement of Andrews’ opponent, Sen. Darline Graham. Trump also plans to hold a campaign rally in South Carolina before the midterms. And the top Senate Republican super PAC is now spending $5 million in the state to shore up Graham’s position. Just a reminder: This is a statewide race in a state where Trump beat Harris by 18 points. If the midterm polls are overstating Democrats’ position heading into November, well, the GOP’s internal polls must be, too. ·      We don’t want to single out retiring North Carolina Sen. Thom Tillis. There’s a bad incentive system out there in which those who at least wrestle with whether to confirm a bad nominee get more criticism than those who don’t apply critical thinking at all. But Tillis, in promoting his new book, has described his deciding vote to confirm Pete Hegseth as Defense secretary as his “biggest regret.” He added, though, that “based on the data I had at the time, I’d do the same thing again.” What other “data” did he need? Hegseth was a weekend television co-host who had published a book the previous year titled Here Are the Ways I’d Be an Asshole If I Ran the Military (paraphrasing). Prior to that, he had mismanaged running a charity, had been married 47 times (estimating), and faced serious accusations about his drinking and treatment of women. And this man was nominated to lead the American military. It wasn’t a tough decision. ·      The Atlantic reported that Trump was having his people draw up plans for attacks on Iran before the midterms, and shortly thereafter, Trump posted that “we will not be attacking Iran at any time prior to the Midterm Elections to be held in the United States on November 3rd.” This is an interesting way for the commander-in-chief to tie his own hands on the use of force for a set period of time. It also overtly concedes that the war timeline is being dictated by the political calendar. Last, it reinforces that he probably does have plans for after the midterms. ·      We’re just tickled by how every day or so, some new audio of Texas Senate candidate Ken Paxton at a private event leaks. You can set your watch to it. That doesn’t mean that what he’s caught saying is particularly interesting, though. When he said that every Republican candidate’s numbers dropped after the GOP midterm convention, that was simply true. When he said that it’s “the war, it’s gas prices, it’s diesel” dragging his candidacy: Ding, ding, ding! When he said that “making some reasonable cuts to mandatory spending—you’re gonna have to do it”—well, yes, that’s the Republican Party position and one that the GOP legislated on just last year with its cuts to Medicaid and SNAP. Did he say anything truly new at this fundraiser, like that he thinks James Talarico is a pretty good guy or that he enjoys wearing dresses when mowing the lawn? We await the next clip … Correction Oct. 10, 2026: This piece has been updated to clarify a GOP Indiana senate candidate’s 2012 remarks on rape and abortion. The Antidote to Our Anti-Democratic Supreme Court

The Antidote to Our Anti-Democratic Supreme Court

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-10

Visit Original Website
America needs bold ideas to break the Supreme Court’s grip on democracy. Enter … the shadow court. Please enable javascript to get your Slate Plus feeds. Donald Trump brags about his boundless freedom while the same Supreme Court that handed him that power has curtailed everyone else’s. Confidence in the court has cratered to historic lows, and waiting for a justice to retire is not actually a plan. Could a “shadow court”––a body set up to critique and even to rival SCOTUS—rescue democracy? In her new book, The Shadow Court: Rescuing Democracy from the Supreme Court, Fordham law professor Julie Suk argues that the court isn’t just making some bad decisions—it’s working exactly as designed. Drawing on constitutional courts in democracies around the world that rebuilt the rule of law after falling to fascism and authoritarianism, Suk outlines a bold institutional answer: a shadow court that would issue advisory opinions on the same issues the Supreme Court decides, giving lawmakers and the public a concrete alternative to judicial supremacy. Dahlia and Julie also discuss how America’s legal and constitutional systems continue to fail women (also by design), from the stalled Equal Rights Amendment to Justice Alito’s claim that women’s reliance on Roe was merely “abstract”—and why the path to real change might run through state ratifying conventions, rather than state and federal institutions that have failed to reform themselves. There’s a Reason Pete Hegseth Chose the Fort Hood Shooter for His Grotesque Publicity Stunt
2026-10-09

There’s a Reason Pete Hegseth Chose the Fort Hood Shooter for His Grotesque Publicity Stunt

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a weekly newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. Execution by firing squad is an especially brutal form of the death penalty. A promise to publicize the execution to the masses reeks of a bygone era of American disgrace, amplifying that brutality. On Thursday, the Pentagon announced a plan to do just that when it carries out the execution of Nidal Hasan. Hasan was a member of the military when he murdered 13 soldiers at Fort Hood in Texas. Secretary of Defense Pete Hegseth has scheduled Hasan’s execution for Dec. 3. “Of course we’re going to do it at Fort Hood,” Hegseth crowed. “And do it on a Thursday and we’re going to do it at 1 o’clock—which is when he did it—and it’s going to be soldiers with rifles executing the firing squad.” The plan to livestream the proceedings is unprecedented in the United States. As the New York Times explains, “Releasing video of executions is most often associated with extremist militant groups like the Islamic State. …There are few countries that hold public executions.” The most prominent of those countries is Iran. Amnesty International is right to say that “executions in public serve no legitimate purpose and only increase the cruel, inhuman and degrading nature of this punishment.” That is precisely the point of the Trump administration’s plan for Hasan’s execution. Hegseth says that he wants the American public to watch because they need to understand the “serious consequences” for Hasan’s crime. But livestreaming Hasan’s execution will not be about transparency or a desire to help the American public better understand what the government does in our name. Instead, it will be the kind of spectacle of cruelty that has become a staple in the administration’s use of power, like having masked Immigration and Customs Enforcement agents roaming American streets or proudly posting videos of the American military killing people in boats off the coast of Venezuela. Public executions were once a staple of American life. From the beginning of the republic, they were carried out in town squares across the country. They were supposed to be moments of public education when citizens could witness justice being done and in which the condemned would deliver a “gallows speech,” acknowledging the sinfulness of the crime and repenting for what they had done. Parents and teachers would often bring children along to teach them a lesson. The events would sometimes feature a choir, a preacher giving an “execution sermon” and the condemned, who was typically a man being hanged, giving a long speech to the crowd. Occasionally executioners even achieved a kind of celebrity status. Arkansas’ George Maledon was so well-known that he was dubbed the “Prince of Hangmen.” Throughout Maledon’s career as a hangman, Arkansas newspapers regularly reported his identity and his role in executions. But by 1936 when Kentucky carried out the last public execution, all that changed. Their educative purpose had given way to a circus-like atmosphere with thousands of people assembling to witness a gruesome event, often tinged with the brutal white supremacy of the Jim Crow south. The New York Times recalls the event, which included an audience of 20,000 people, this way: Kentucky was the only state still conducting public hangings, a fate reserved for what was viewed by Kentucky’s citizens as the most heinous crime: rape. The condemned was a young black man named Rainey Bethea. He had been convicted of raping an elderly white woman, a wealthy widow who was found choked to death in her bedroom. Bethea’s execution carried with it the taint of a lynching as had previous public executions. And these executions seemed simply uncivilized, the sort of thing that decent people would not want to see. Once new execution technologies like the electric chair became the preferred execution method, putting people to death in the town square was no longer feasible. Scholars have claimed that there is something very different about the visceral experience of witnessing the state putting someone to death and the experience of watching that happen on television or a video feed. Wendy Lesser argues that such a viewing experience would be voyeuristic, allowing viewers to see from a safe distance something that is itself shameful. What Hegseth intends to do would indeed be shameful. It would be the equivalent of the government producing and distributing a snuff film, a film that captures a real killing and uses it to appeal to viewers’ sadistic impulses. Unlike lethal injection, the effects of which are often hidden behind a medicalized facade, the brutality of the firing squad is exactly what Hegseth wants Americans to see. Hasan will be seated in a chair with a hood placed over his head with a paper heart affixed to his chest. A group of anonymous men, each carrying identical rifles, will stand behind a wall cut with gun ports and on command shoot their high-powered rifles. The damage done to Hasan’s body will be devastating. That’s why even Vice President J.D. Vance says he won’t watch it. As though a nod to those racist public spectacles of yore, Hasan’s Muslim background almost certainly explains why Hegseth is so determined that he be the first to endure such a punishment in such a public way. In the end, though, a brutal public execution will not bring honor to the American military or to this country. It will unfortunately only confirm the widespread view that our country has lost its mind and abandoned any pretense to decency in its dealings with its citizens, or those of other nations. All of us will pay the price for Hegseth’s unspeakably vile publicity stunt. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. If you value our work, please disable your ad blocker. The Saving Grace of Trump’s Revenge Tour
2026-10-09

The Saving Grace of Trump’s Revenge Tour

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. Just eight days into Donald Trump’s first term, the legal expert Benjamin Wittes coined a phrase that would come to describe many of the president’s actions: “malevolence tempered by incompetence.” Wittes’ idea was that Trump’s inattention to detail and ignorance of how government works often undermine his malign aims. More than nine years later, the phrase still applies—this time to Trump’s efforts to use the power of the federal government to punish his political enemies. A president determined to weaponize his office is, obviously, a dangerous perversion of how American presidents should behave. Yet two new investigations reveal both the malevolence and the incompetence of this president’s ongoing revenge tour. The first effort reflects one of Trump’s main preoccupations: his 2020 election defeat. After Trump claimed fraud and egged on the crowd that sacked the U.S. Capitol on Jan. 6, some of his own officials turned against him. One of them was Cassidy Hutchinson, a former aide to Trump’s then–chief of staff, who testified to a House committee investigating the attack that Trump wanted magnetometers near the White House removed to let potentially armed rioters join the crowd. On Thursday, we learned that Trump’s Justice Department is reportedly trying to convince a grand jury in D.C. to charge Hutchinson with a crime or crimes, which could include lying to Congress. It’s not yet clear what prosecutors think Hutchinson lied about, but threatening a former subordinate with jail time in an attempt to rewrite the history of your coup attempt is as nefarious as it gets. And while it’s certainly possible that the grand jury will indict Hutchinson, several of Trump’s other efforts to turn the justice system against his enemies have fallen flat. Grand juries in Washington have refused to criminally charge Democratic lawmakers who urged service members to disobey illegal orders, a protester accused of throwing a sandwich at a federal immigration agent, and several others. Still other indictments—against New York Attorney General Letitia James and former FBI Director James Comey—have fallen apart under judges’ scrutiny. The DOJ itself has also struggled to find pretexts on which to hang indictments of other Trump foes and is hemorrhaging staff as career prosecutors balk at taking part in nakedly political investigations. Unlike in his first term, Trump is surrounded today by true believers who are earnestly trying to realize his revenge fantasies. But the law keeps thwarting them, and the Hutchinson case could become the latest example. Still, the president has shown a dogged willingness to try, try again. His second effort to go after a political foe this week is actually a redo of an earlier one. On Friday, Trump announced a White House committee to investigate Lisa Cook, a Federal Reserve governor, over allegations of mortgage fraud leveled by one of the president’s own appointees. The committee, which is stocked with Trump appointees, is set to hold a hearing next month about whether Cook should lose her job. Trump, who has accused the Fed of political bias, seems to be gunning for Cook in part because she’s a Joe Biden appointee. But he’s also targeting her now because his previous attempt to fire her flopped. In a rare adverse ruling for the president, the Supreme Court temporarily blocked Trump from removing Cook in June, saying she hadn’t had sufficient opportunity to contest the claims against her (she denies them). But a majority of the justices also signaled that they don’t like the idea of presidents removing Fed officials on a whim because the central bank’s independence is key to setting sound monetary policy. So even though Trump’s new committee looks like a kangaroo court, Cook’s future is still very much in doubt. One reason the president’s revenge tour keeps flailing is that it’s largely a product of his delusions, which keep running smack into the brick wall of reality. But the possibility that Trump will fail to nail both of his latest targets doesn’t mean what he’s doing is benign. The mere fact of being investigated can exact a toll in stress and legal fees, and even watching Trump fall short could discourage others from standing up to him. We’re also less than halfway through Trump’s second term, and if his first is any indication, things could get rockier toward the end. If Democrats retake Congress next month, it may leave Trump more isolated and inclined to lash out. Buckle up! Slate was an early adopter of the listicle, that now-familiar internet format. Over the years, the magazine has ranked everything from Super Bowl halftime shows and horrible wedding songs to pre-YouTube viral hits and Declaration of Independence signatures. In honor of the magazine’s 30th birthday, my colleagues have compiled a list of lists to keep you company over the long weekend: All 339 Olympic events: The next Olympics is still a couple years away, but it’s not too early to start brushing up on your knowledge of the Summer Games. Before the torch is lit in Los Angeles in 2028, make sure to consult Slate’s list of every Olympic event, ranked from worst to best, compiled by Mike Canter. (For those who prefer the Winter Games, Justin Peters has you covered, with rankings of the best and worst sports—and the events ranked by the likelihood of a dog winning.) The 40 greatest stand-alone TV episodes of all time: If you want to watch some TV this weekend but aren’t sure where to start, Slate has you covered. In this list, our writers recommend the best episodes that you can dive into right away—without having to commit to the whole series. It’s a great way to sample a new show. All 349 “murmurs” in the Twilight saga: In this very scientific investigation, Rachelle Hampton ranked and charted all the times Stephenie Meyer used the word murmur in her YA vampire series. Come for the many murmured quotes, stay for some top-notch literary analysis. The three things you need to know about politics this week: Slate senior politics writer Jim Newell is here to give you a sanity check. In his newsletter, the Surge, Jim explains one story that’s essential for understanding the news, one story to keep an eye on, and one story that everyone needs to chill out about. Sign up to get the Surge in your inbox every Saturday! We’re off Monday for the holiday, so we’ll see you back here on Tuesday. Enjoy the long weekend! Kathy Hochul Says New York Needs to Close the “Cornell Seven” Loophole. That’s False.
2026-10-09

Kathy Hochul Says New York Needs to Close the “Cornell Seven” Loophole. That’s False.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-09

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a weekly newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. A civil lawsuit alleging binge drinking, rampant drugging, and gang rape at Cornell has sparked a firestorm on social media and front-page headlines. The incident occurred two years ago. Since then, the university has investigated the “Cornell Seven”—the seven frat brothers directly involved—and imposed discipline ranging from expulsion to compulsory essay writing. For many, this should never have been a campus matter but one for our criminal justice system. There has been particular ire for the reported criminal law “loophole” that the district attorney claimed tied his hands in this case, preventing initial charges. This is a red herring. Even with the current law, prosecutors had everything they needed to charge the alleged perpetrators in this case and potentially win conviction. Allow me to explain. Politicians have reacted predictably to this case with claims that criminal law is not tough enough, and prosecutors need more tools. Slamming Tompkins County District Attorney Matthew Van Houten for declining to charge the men, Gov. Kathy Hochul urged the Legislature to “close the loophole” that keeps prosecutors from convicting people who have sex with the intoxicated and the insufficiently participatory. I recall a time not too long ago when progressives and campus feminists agitated for reducing mass incarceration and the reach of America’s racially biased policing and punishment system. But such institutional concerns evaporate in the face of stories that invoke justifiable outrage against the nauseating misogyny of fraternity culture but also invite gendered ideas about women’s sexual vulnerability, ruination, and squeamishness over sexual impropriety. The impulse to avoid acknowledging that victims chose any part of a harmful sexual encounter produces overbroad criminal laws that leave little room for female sexual agency. To be clear, there are no intoxication or consent “loopholes” in New York sexual assault law. New York’s regime prohibits forcing others into sex (“rape”), imposing sex against their will (“sexual misconduct”), and having sex with “physically helpless” people (“rape”). If members of the Cornell Seven used force or threats to compel sex, or even just proceeded without Jane Doe’s consent, they committed a crime, regardless of whether she or they were drunk. Contrary to the DA’s analysis, New York law does not require unequivocal verbal refusal for sex to be nonconsensual. Perusing the DA’s justification for declining prosecution, one readily sees facts that could support charges. Jane Doe, surrounded by larger men, felt intimidated and unable to leave. She swatted hands away and said “no”; she hid under covers and became “9 out of 10” intoxicated. The DA could have brought a criminal case. We need not create a new crime of sex without an “affirmative expression of consent”—one that would render consensual, even desired, but “yes”-less sex criminal—to get at the wrongdoing alleged here. The same can be said about intoxication. Sex with a person intoxicated to the point of helplessness is already a crime—in fact, first-degree rape. A drunk but conscious person can be “physically helpless” if intoxication leaves them “unable to communicate unwillingness”—a factual determination New York courts have left to the jury. Jane Doe had been drinking heavily when she arrived at the frat house, but she was able to consider and agree to the threesome. The epistemic mismatch between her idea of what would transpire and the frat brothers’ is truly tragic. Doe had reportedly intended to hook up with Matthew Ingalls and agreed for Jonathan Newell to join in, “excited” by the prospect that they might be gay. Far from fulfilling a Heated Rivalry fantasy with male friends who “had her back,” she entered a dystopia of manosphere misogyny. Based on Doe’s legal complaint, she became the victim of a fraternity culture bent on getting one over on women as an act of toxic homosocial bonding, as evidenced by a group chat that attempted to reduce her to “free pussy.” Indeed, research finds that college men often have sex they don’t want because of pressure to conform to masculinist norms. Doe’s friends-with-benefits scenario shattered, according to the complaint, as the situation grew increasingly coercive, nonconsensual, and she approached or crossed the threshold into intoxicated helplessness. Unfortunately, the current conversation, including the call to broaden criminal law, accepts the dyad where a woman is either a complicit “slut” or a helpless, and later ruined, victim. The men’s defenders insist that Doe is the true deviant who eagerly agreed to kinky—even painfully kinky—sex, then lied about it because she was shunned from a party. A strain of “consent to some is consent to all” even runs through the DA’s explanation for his decision not to charge. But the outraged rejoinder has not generally emphasized that women can be sexual agents who agree to some activity and are assaulted in the same encounter, or that unwise and unpleasant but consensual sex is different from, yet can cross into, criminally wrongful sex. Instead, the lawsuit and many internet pundits deny that Doe had the capacity to make choices at all. She moved through the world requiring protection from drinking, from going to meet a guy she was interested in, and from agreeing to unconventional sex. So eager are online feminists to spare Doe the stigma of the girl in the threesome—a stigma we should be fighting, not reinforcing—that they insist that, despite Doe’s reasonable rationale for agreeing to it, she could not have been thinking straight. Doe herself described the binary, recalling reading an account that she was “gang raped” and thinking, “That’s the first time I had ever heard the narrative change that I wasn’t a whore and that I was assaulted.” So what exactly do Hochul and company mean by “closing the intoxication loophole”? Do they want to criminalize sex with someone who is intoxicated but appears lucid and able to agree, as Doe initially did? Given that neither a defendant’s own drunkenness nor their reasonable belief that the other consented is a defense, it behooves us to be clear about how drunk is too drunk, because that is the only line that matters. If both parties are sufficiently intoxicated when they have sex, each is a victim and a perpetrator. That both believe the sex was mutually desired is immaterial. Making intoxicated but not incapacitated sex a crime would grant prosecutors enormous authority, given how often drinking and sexual activity go together, especially among the young. Which groups will constitute the “bad guys” against whom prosecutors wield their newly minted power to prosecute drunk sex—and will they look like the Cornell Seven? Broadening criminal law to encompass widespread sexual behavior should happen only after forthright public debate. It should not be done in a viral reactive moment and couched as merely closing a loophole that doesn’t even exist. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism.
